[Director 指令] [Patch — 高优先级] **Sign-reversal 修正数据已就绪（τ=0.03），更新论文**

Reviewer 已 PASS 修正版数据（genuine CATE sign flip，不再是 bias direction flip）。

## 关键数字更新

| τ(0) | Global CATE | True CATE | 符号翻转 |
|------|-------------|-----------|---------|
| 0.01 | **-0.079** | +0.01 | ✅ 完全错误 |
| 0.03 | **-0.059** | +0.03 | ✅ 完全错误 |
| 0.05 | **-0.039** | +0.05 | ✅ 完全错误 |
| 0.10 | >0 | +0.10 | ❌ 无翻转 |

- Critical threshold: τ(0) < 0.0893 时发生符号翻转（精确匹配 Theorem 1：δ_max · Δτ_max / (1-2π̄) = 0.125 × 0.50 / 0.70）
- τ=0.03 时 14.9% 配置发生 sign reversal（约 1/7）
- τ=0.01 时 31% 配置发生 sign reversal（近 1/3）
- EC-HTE: **100% 保持正确符号**

## 需要更新的位置

1. **Figure 1**（sign-reversal 3-panel）：用修正版 figure（τ=0.03 数据）替换当前版本。Agent 已生成新的 `sign_reversal_figure.pdf`。
2. **Abstract**: 当前写 "reverses the sign of estimated treatment effects for at least one subgroup in 92% of configurations"——这个 92% 是原始分析（bias flip），需确认是否仍适用于 τ=0.03 设置，或更新为 τ-specific 数字。
3. **Intro P2 shock 段**: 加入标志性例子——"global correction concludes a protective treatment (true τ = +0.03) is harmful (estimated τ = −0.059)"
4. **Experiments §5.2**: 更新 sign-reversal 统计为修正版数字
5. **Conclusion**: 确保 sign-reversal 描述与修正数据一致

## 叙事升级

之前的叙事："global correction flips the bias direction"
**新叙事**："global correction leads to the wrong policy conclusion — a beneficial treatment appears harmful"

这是从统计现象升级为政策后果，直接提 Excitement。

## 注意

Now I'm drafting the experimental results section, trying to balance precision with clarity—I want to cite the subgroup-level counts while anchoring the main claim to configuration-level percentages, and I'm working through how to present this without overcomplicating the narrative. The intro example is already working well for the narrative, so I'll keep it as is. The Director wants me to add a concrete numerical instance showing how global correction can flip a protective effect into a harmful one—something like a true treatment effect of +0.03 becoming an estimated −0.059—and I've woven that into the second paragraph. Now I'm executing the full set of edits across the abstract, introduction sections, experiments, and conclusion to integrate the new quantitative findings throughout the paper. _source line 6569 · 2026-05-23T15:42:21.394Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016pKYK9PYw6zj2i1xD5orsp`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Standard confusion-matrix correction for noisy annotations assumes uniform error rates.
3	When this assumption fails, correction can increase rather than decrease bias in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) estimates, reversing the sign of estimated effect heterogeneity.
4	LLMs, now widely used as proxy classifiers in computational social science, are the leading practical instance, with error rates varying by up to $5\times$ across demographic subgroups.
5	We derive the first exact bias decomposition for this setting: global correction introduces subgroup-level bias with a product form factoring into each subgroup's deviation from the pooled error rate, the treatment effect heterogeneity, and an amplification factor from matrix inversion.
6	This is not a bound but a population-level identity; global correction can reverse the sign of estimated CATE for at least one subgroup in {\bfseries[XX]\%} of configurations, and in all eight real LLMs tested.
7	The decomposition yields a diagnostic that predicts harmful correction from per-subgroup error rates alone (AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity).
8	Error-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects (EC-HTE), the per-subgroup correction prescribed by the decomposition, reduces $|\text{bias}|$ by $7.4\times$ under extreme heterogeneity with coverage $\geq 0.90$ via hierarchical Bayesian estimation; the advantage requires sufficient error heterogeneity and expert budget.
9	\end{abstract}
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:intro}
3	
4	Consider estimating whether a content moderation policy reduces hate speech differently across demographic groups, using GPT-4 to classify thousands of posts.
5	After standard confusion-matrix correction, the analysis concludes the policy \emph{increases} hate speech among younger users---but the true effect is protective.
6	This sign reversal is not an edge case.
7	
8	Large language models (LLMs) now routinely replace human annotators in computational social science (CSS), producing labels for sentiment, hate speech, stance, and political discourse at costs orders of magnitude below manual coding~\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp}.
9	When these labels serve as effect modifiers in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) analysis, the standard correction protocol\footnote{Throughout, ``debiasing'' refers to correction of annotation measurement-error bias, not algorithmic fairness debiasing.} is straightforward: validate a sample against expert annotations, estimate a confusion matrix, and apply its inverse uniformly to correct conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimates.
10	Yet LLM annotations are not uniformly accurate: \citet{ashwin2025bias} document misclassification rates varying by up to 5$\times$ across demographic subgroups defined by refugee status, gender, and education level; in our pilot study, GPT-4.1 achieves 85.8\% accuracy on TweetEval sentiment, but error rates range from 10.2\% to 20.0\% across text-feature subgroups ($\Delta\pi = 0.098$).
11	
12	We show that this standard correction can make things worse.
13	In {\bfseries[XX]\%} of our synthetic configurations, global correction reverses the sign of the estimated CATE for at least one subgroup, directing resources toward communities that benefit least (Figure~\ref{fig:sign_reversal}); all eight real LLMs tested exhibit sign reversal in {\bfseries[YY]\%} of subgroup estimates, while EC-HTE eliminates it entirely.
14	Global correction amplifies bias by up to 5.9$\times$ ($K{=}4$ with CausalForest); the classical Frazis-Loeb scalar attenuation correction~\cite{frazis2003estimating} \emph{increases} bias by up to 2.9$\times$.
15	The mechanism is over-correction: for subgroups with near-zero misclassification, the naive estimator is nearly unbiased, yet pooled correction calibrated for the average error rate introduces substantial bias precisely where labels were most trustworthy.
16	
17	We derive an exact bias identity explaining when and why this occurs (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}): the correction-induced bias in each subgroup factors into three independently estimable components, verified empirically at $R^2 \geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and $R^2 = 0.975$ across 11 real LLM confusion matrices spanning open-source and proprietary model families.
18	The identity yields a diagnostic (Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}) that predicts when global correction will harm a subgroup using only per-subgroup misclassification rates, without estimating any treatment effects (AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity; Section~\ref{sec:diagnostic}).
19	
20	Error-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects (EC-HTE), the per-subgroup correction prescribed by the identity, reduces $|\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.008 under extreme heterogeneity ($K{=}2$) via hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation.
21	EC-HTE's advantage is conditional on sufficient error heterogeneity and expert budget; when $\Delta\pi$ is small, the variance cost of stratification outweighs its bias benefit.
22	
23	Existing corrections---PPI~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi}, DSL~\cite{egami2024dsl}, PPCI~\cite{sui2026ppci}, arm-specific PPI~\cite{mozer2026ppi}, ForestIV~\cite{bu2023forestiv}---assume homogeneous prediction error; none addresses effect modifier misclassification (Section~\ref{sec:related}).
24	
25	\begin{figure*}[t]
26	\centering
27	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig1_sign_reversal.pdf}
28	\caption{CATE sign reversal under global correction ($K{=}2$). (A) Naive vs.\ global-corrected CATE; red triangles mark sign reversals ({\bfseries[ZZ]\%} of subgroup estimates). (B) Three extreme cases. (C) Summary: {\bfseries[XX]\%} of configurations and all 8 real LLMs exhibit reversal; EC-HTE: 0\%.}
29	\label{fig:sign_reversal}
30	\end{figure*}
31	
32	Our contributions are:
33	\begin{itemize}
34	    \item A demonstration that standard confusion-matrix correction---the default in PPI, DSL, and CSS practice---can reverse the sign of estimated CATE in {\bfseries[XX]\%} of configurations and in all eight real LLMs tested; we derive the first exact finite-population bias decomposition (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}) explaining when and why, with multiplicative structure $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau / (1-2\bar{\pi})$, verified at $R^2 \geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and 11 real LLM confusion matrices (Sections~\ref{sec:theory},~\ref{sec:bias_mechanism}).
35	    \item An actionable diagnostic derived from the decomposition (Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}): a condition on per-subgroup misclassification rates alone predicts when global correction increases bias, achieving AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity---independently useful without adopting EC-HTE (Section~\ref{sec:diagnostic}).
36	    \item EC-HTE, an end-to-end correction pipeline: the bias decomposition identifies the correction target, the diagnostic flags when correction is needed, and hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation executes per-subgroup correction---compatible with difference-in-means, CausalForest, and R-learner, and composable with PPCI for joint covariate/outcome error (Sections~\ref{sec:method}--\ref{sec:diagnostic}; Appendix~\ref{sec:budget}).
37	\end{itemize}
38	While we develop EC-HTE for LLM annotation, the bias identity applies to any proxy classifier with subgroup-varying error rates---survey coding, medical diagnosis, and satellite imagery classification all share this structure.
39	
```

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nStandard confusion-matrix correction for noisy annotations assumes uniform error rates.\nWhen this assumption fails, correction can increase rather than decrease bias in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) estimates, reversing the sign of estimated effect heterogeneity.\nLLMs, now widely used as proxy classifiers in computational social science, are the leading practical instance, with error rates varying by up to $5\\times$ across demographic subgroups.\nWe derive the first exact bias decomposition for this setting: global correction introduces subgroup-level bias with a product form factoring into each subgroup's deviation from the pooled error rate, the treatment effect heterogeneity, and an amplification factor from matrix inversion.\nThis is not a bound but a population-level identity; global correction can reverse the sign of estimated CATE for at least one subgroup in {\\bfseries[XX]\\%} of configurations, and in all eight real LLMs tested.\nThe decomposition yields a diagnostic that predicts harmful correction from per-subgroup error rates alone (AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity).\nError-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects (EC-HTE), the per-subgroup correction prescribed by the decomposition, reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ by $7.4\\times$ under extreme heterogeneity with coverage $\\geq 0.90$ via hierarchical Bayesian estimation; the advantage requires sufficient error heterogeneity and expert budget.\n\\end{abstract}",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520/projects/ec_hte_llm_annotation/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "In {\\bfseries[XX]\\%} of our synthetic configurations, global correction reverses the sign of the estimated CATE for at least one subgroup, directing resources toward communities that benefit least (Figure~\\ref{fig:sign_reversal}); all eight real LLMs tested exhibit sign reversal in {\\bfseries[YY]\\%} of subgroup estimates, while EC-HTE eliminates it entirely.",
    "newString": "In 14.9\\% of our synthetic configurations ($\\tau(0) = 0.03$), global correction reverses the sign of estimated CATE---a protective treatment ($\\tau = +0.03$) appears harmful ($\\hat{\\tau} = -0.059$)---rising to 31\\% at $\\tau(0) = 0.01$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:sign_reversal}); all eight real LLMs tested exhibit reversal, while EC-HTE eliminates it entirely.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nConsider estimating whether a content moderation policy reduces hate speech differently across demographic groups, using GPT-4 to classify thousands of posts.\nAfter standard confusion-matrix correction, the analysis concludes the policy \\emph{increases} hate speech among younger users---but the true effect is protective.\nThis sign reversal is not an edge case.\n\nLarge language models (LLMs) now routinely replace human annotators in computational social science (CSS), producing labels for sentiment, hate speech, stance, and political discourse at costs orders of magnitude below manual coding~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp}.\nWhen these labels serve as effect modifiers in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) analysis, the standard correction protocol\\footnote{Throughout, ``debiasing'' refers to correction of annotation measurement-error bias, not algorithmic fairness debiasing.} is straightforward: validate a sample against expert annotations, estimate a confusion matrix, and apply its inverse uniformly to correct conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimates.\nYet LLM annotations are not uniformly accurate: \\citet{ashwin2025bias} document misclassification rates varying by up to 5$\\times$ across demographic subgroups defined by refugee status, gender, and education level; in our pilot study, GPT-4.1 achieves 85.8\\% accuracy on TweetEval sentiment, but error rates range from 10.2\\% to 20.0\\% across text-feature subgroups ($\\Delta\\pi = 0.098$).\n\nWe show that this standard correction can make things worse.\nIn {\\bfseries[XX]\\%} of our synthetic configurations, global correction reverses the sign of the estimated CATE for at least one subgroup, directing resources toward communities that benefit least (Figure~\\ref{fig:sign_reversal}); all eight real LLMs tested exhibit sign reversal in {\\bfseries[YY]\\%} of subgroup estimates, while EC-HTE eliminates it entirely.\nGlobal correction amplifies bias by up to 5.9$\\times$ ($K{=}4$ with CausalForest); the classical Frazis-Loeb scalar attenuation correction~\\cite{frazis2003estimating} \\emph{increases} bias by up to 2.9$\\times$.\nThe mechanism is over-correction: for subgroups with near-zero misclassification, the naive estimator is nearly unbiased, yet pooled correction calibrated for the average error rate introduces substantial bias precisely where labels were most trustworthy.\n\nWe derive an exact bias identity explaining when and why this occurs (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}): the correction-induced bias in each subgroup factors into three independently estimable components, verified empirically at $R^2 \\geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and $R^2 = 0.975$ across 11 real LLM confusion matrices spanning open-source and proprietary model families.\nThe identity yields a diagnostic (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}) that predicts when global correction will harm a subgroup using only per-subgroup misclassification rates, without estimating any treatment effects (AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity; Section~\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\n\nError-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects (EC-HTE), the per-subgroup correction prescribed by the identity, reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.008 under extreme heterogeneity ($K{=}2$) via hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation.\nEC-HTE's advantage is conditional on sufficient error heterogeneity and expert budget; when $\\Delta\\pi$ is small, the variance cost of stratification outweighs its bias benefit.\n\nExisting corrections---PPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi}, DSL~\\cite{egami2024dsl}, PPCI~\\cite{sui2026ppci}, arm-specific PPI~\\cite{mozer2026ppi}, ForestIV~\\cite{bu2023forestiv}---assume homogeneous prediction error; none addresses effect modifier misclassification (Section~\\ref{sec:related}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_sign_reversal.pdf}\n\\caption{CATE sign reversal under global correction ($K{=}2$). (A) Naive vs.\\ global-corrected CATE; red triangles mark sign reversals ({\\bfseries[ZZ]\\%} of subgroup estimates). (B) Three extreme cases. (C) Summary: {\\bfseries[XX]\\%} of configurations and all 8 real LLMs exhibit reversal; EC-HTE: 0\\%.}\n\\label{fig:sign_reversal}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item A demonstration that standard confusion-matrix correction---the default in PPI, DSL, and CSS practice---can reverse the sign of estimated CATE in {\\bfseries[XX]\\%} of configurations and in all eight real LLMs tested; we derive the first exact finite-population bias decomposition (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}) explaining when and why, with multiplicative structure $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau / (1-2\\bar{\\pi})$, verified at $R^2 \\geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and 11 real LLM confusion matrices (Sections~\\ref{sec:theory},~\\ref{sec:bias_mechanism}).\n    \\item An actionable diagnostic derived from the decomposition (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}): a condition on per-subgroup misclassification rates alone predicts when global correction increases bias, achieving AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity---independently useful without adopting EC-HTE (Section~\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\n    \\item EC-HTE, an end-to-end correction pipeline: the bias decomposition identifies the correction target, the diagnostic flags when correction is needed, and hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation executes per-subgroup correction---compatible with difference-in-means, CausalForest, and R-learner, and composable with PPCI for joint covariate/outcome error (Sections~\\ref{sec:method}--\\ref{sec:diagnostic}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:budget}).\n\\end{itemize}\nWhile we develop EC-HTE for LLM annotation, the bias identity applies to any proxy classifier with subgroup-varying error rates---survey coding, medical diagnosis, and satellite imagery classification all share this structure.\n",

    "oldString": "    \\item A demonstration that standard confusion-matrix correction---the default in PPI, DSL, and CSS practice---can reverse the sign of estimated CATE in {\\bfseries[XX]\\%} of configurations and in all eight real LLMs tested; we derive the first exact finite-population bias decomposition (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}) explaining when and why, with multiplicative structure $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau / (1-2\\bar{\\pi})$, verified at $R^2 \\geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and 11 real LLM confusion matrices (Sections~\\ref{sec:theory},~\\ref{sec:bias_mechanism}).",
    "newString": "    \\item A demonstration that standard confusion-matrix correction---the default in PPI, DSL, and CSS practice---can reverse the sign of estimated CATE in 14.9\\% of configurations ($\\tau(0) = 0.03$; 31\\% at $\\tau(0) = 0.01$) and in all eight real LLMs tested; we derive the first exact finite-population bias decomposition (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}) explaining when and why, with multiplicative structure $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau / (1-2\\bar{\\pi})$, verified at $R^2 \\geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and 11 real LLM confusion matrices (Sections~\\ref{sec:theory},~\\ref{sec:bias_mechanism}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nConsider estimating whether a content moderation policy reduces hate speech differently across demographic groups, using GPT-4 to classify thousands of posts.\nAfter standard confusion-matrix correction, the analysis concludes the policy \\emph{increases} hate speech among younger users---but the true effect is protective.\nThis sign reversal is not an edge case.\n\nLarge language models (LLMs) now routinely replace human annotators in computational social science (CSS), producing labels for sentiment, hate speech, stance, and political discourse at costs orders of magnitude below manual coding~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp}.\nWhen these labels serve as effect modifiers in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) analysis, the standard correction protocol\\footnote{Throughout, ``debiasing'' refers to correction of annotation measurement-error bias, not algorithmic fairness debiasing.} is straightforward: validate a sample against expert annotations, estimate a confusion matrix, and apply its inverse uniformly to correct conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimates.\nYet LLM annotations are not uniformly accurate: \\citet{ashwin2025bias} document misclassification rates varying by up to 5$\\times$ across demographic subgroups defined by refugee status, gender, and education level; in our pilot study, GPT-4.1 achieves 85.8\\% accuracy on TweetEval sentiment, but error rates range from 10.2\\% to 20.0\\% across text-feature subgroups ($\\Delta\\pi = 0.098$).\n\nWe show that this standard correction can make things worse.\nIn 14.9\\% of our synthetic configurations ($\\tau(0) = 0.03$), global correction reverses the sign of estimated CATE---a protective treatment ($\\tau = +0.03$) appears harmful ($\\hat{\\tau} = -0.059$)---rising to 31\\% at $\\tau(0) = 0.01$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:sign_reversal}); all eight real LLMs tested exhibit reversal, while EC-HTE eliminates it entirely.\nGlobal correction amplifies bias by up to 5.9$\\times$ ($K{=}4$ with CausalForest); the classical Frazis-Loeb scalar attenuation correction~\\cite{frazis2003estimating} \\emph{increases} bias by up to 2.9$\\times$.\nThe mechanism is over-correction: for subgroups with near-zero misclassification, the naive estimator is nearly unbiased, yet pooled correction calibrated for the average error rate introduces substantial bias precisely where labels were most trustworthy.\n\nWe derive an exact bias identity explaining when and why this occurs (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}): the correction-induced bias in each subgroup factors into three independently estimable components, verified empirically at $R^2 \\geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and $R^2 = 0.975$ across 11 real LLM confusion matrices spanning open-source and proprietary model families.\nThe identity yields a diagnostic (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}) that predicts when global correction will harm a subgroup using only per-subgroup misclassification rates, without estimating any treatment effects (AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity; Section~\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\n\nError-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects (EC-HTE), the per-subgroup correction prescribed by the identity, reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.008 under extreme heterogeneity ($K{=}2$) via hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation.\nEC-HTE's advantage is conditional on sufficient error heterogeneity and expert budget; when $\\Delta\\pi$ is small, the variance cost of stratification outweighs its bias benefit.\n\nExisting corrections---PPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi}, DSL~\\cite{egami2024dsl}, PPCI~\\cite{sui2026ppci}, arm-specific PPI~\\cite{mozer2026ppi}, ForestIV~\\cite{bu2023forestiv}---assume homogeneous prediction error; none addresses effect modifier misclassification (Section~\\ref{sec:related}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_sign_reversal.pdf}\n\\caption{CATE sign reversal under global correction ($K{=}2$, $\\tau(0) = 0.03$). (A) Naive vs.\\ global-corrected CATE; red triangles mark sign reversals (7.5\\% of subgroup estimates). (B) Extreme case: true $\\tau = +0.03$, estimated $\\hat{\\tau} = -0.059$. (C) 14.9\\% of configurations and all 8 real LLMs exhibit reversal; EC-HTE: 0\\%.}\n\\label{fig:sign_reversal}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item A demonstration that standard confusion-matrix correction---the default in PPI, DSL, and CSS practice---can reverse the sign of estimated CATE in {\\bfseries[XX]\\%} of configurations and in all eight real LLMs tested; we derive the first exact finite-population bias decomposition (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}) explaining when and why, with multiplicative structure $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau / (1-2\\bar{\\pi})$, verified at $R^2 \\geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and 11 real LLM confusion matrices (Sections~\\ref{sec:theory},~\\ref{sec:bias_mechanism}).\n    \\item An actionable diagnostic derived from the decomposition (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}): a condition on per-subgroup misclassification rates alone predicts when global correction increases bias, achieving AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity---independently useful without adopting EC-HTE (Section~\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\n    \\item EC-HTE, an end-to-end correction pipeline: the bias decomposition identifies the correction target, the diagnostic flags when correction is needed, and hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation executes per-subgroup correction---compatible with difference-in-means, CausalForest, and R-learner, and composable with PPCI for joint covariate/outcome error (Sections~\\ref{sec:method}--\\ref{sec:diagnostic}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:budget}).\n\\end{itemize}\nWhile we develop EC-HTE for LLM annotation, the bias identity applies to any proxy classifier with subgroup-varying error rates---survey coding, medical diagnosis, and satellite imagery classification all share this structure.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe test on synthetic ($N{=}5{,}000$, $K \\in \\{2, 4\\}$, controlled $\\Delta\\tau$ and $\\Delta\\pi$, 100 MC runs) and semi-synthetic (TweetEval sentiment~\\cite{barbieri2020tweeteval}, $K{=}4$ unbalanced subgroups, $\\pi \\in \\{0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.25\\}$) DGPs; full specifications in Appendix~\\ref{app:dgp}.\n\nWe compare: (1) \\textit{Naive} (no correction), (2) \\textit{Global} (pooled confusion matrix, equivalent to PPI/DSL~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,egami2024dsl} with homogeneous error), (3) \\textit{Stratified MLE} (per-subgroup DSL without shrinkage), (4) \\textit{HB EC-HTE} (our method), (5) \\textit{PPCI}~\\cite{sui2026ppci}, and (6) \\textit{EC-HTE+PPCI}.\nFor CausalForest~\\cite{wager2018causal}, we compare naive, global, and EC-HTE variants.\nPrimary metrics are $|\\text{bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\\% CI coverage, averaged across subgroups and 100 MC runs.\nRobustness validation covers hate speech, six OpenAI models, subgroup misspecification, asymmetric confusion matrices, confounded treatment, and outcome-dependent misclassification (all $\\geq$93.5\\% coverage; Appendices~\\ref{app:hatespeech}--\\ref{app:a1_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation of the Bias Identity}\n\\label{sec:bias_mechanism}\n\nWe verify Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity} on a $K{=}2$ grid (520 configurations, $\\Delta\\pi \\in [0, 0.25]$, $\\Delta\\tau \\in (0, 0.50]$) and a $K{=}4$ grid (Appendix~\\ref{app:dgp}).\nRegressing observed subgroup bias on $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau / (1{-}2\\bar{\\pi})$ yields $R^2 = 0.994$ ($K{=}2$) and $0.974$ ($K{=}4$); residual variance reflects MC noise ($R^2 \\geq 0.976$ even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$; Appendix~\\ref{app:classimbalance}).\nGlobal correction worsens bias in 50\\% of $K{=}2$ configurations (260/520, excluding $\\Delta\\pi{=}0$), with amplification reaching 2.86$\\times$ ($K{=}2$) and 2.41$\\times$ ($K{=}4$); Figure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} visualizes the amplification surface.\nBeyond amplification, global correction reverses the sign of the estimated CATE for at least one subgroup in {\\bfseries[XX]\\%} of configurations ({\\bfseries[N/M]}, {\\bfseries[ZZ]\\%} of all subgroup-rows), with all eight real LLMs exhibiting the same pattern ({\\bfseries[YY]\\%}); EC-HTE eliminates sign reversal entirely (Figure~\\ref{fig:sign_reversal}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig2_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Bias amplification under global correction ($K{=}2$). (a) Low-error subgroup: correction always helps. (b) High-error subgroup: correction \\emph{increases} bias when $\\Delta\\pi > 0.12$ (hatched), reaching 2.86$\\times$.}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{EC-HTE Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:ec_hte_eval}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} presents the main results across representative configurations.\nIn the $K{=}2$ extreme row, global correction \\emph{increases} $|\\text{bias}|$ relative to naive (0.059 vs.\\ 0.058), confirming the over-correction mechanism of Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}.\nF-L scalar correction ($\\hat{\\tau}/(1{-}2\\bar{\\pi})$) increases $|\\text{bias}|$ to 0.122 with coverage 0.68---worse than naive---because a single scalar cannot correct heterogeneous attenuation; F-L per-subgroup inflates bias to 0.168 as $(1{-}2\\pi_s) \\to 0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).\nHB EC-HTE reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.008 (7.4$\\times$) at $K{=}2$ and from 0.056 to 0.012 (4.7$\\times$) at $K{=}4$, maintaining near-nominal coverage (0.94--0.96) with lower RMSE than stratified MLE.\nUnder moderate heterogeneity and in TweetEval ($K{=}4$), methods converge ($p > 0.05$); EC-HTE incurs higher RMSE when $\\Delta\\pi$ is small (Table~\\ref{tab:main_supp}).\n\n\\paragraph{CausalForest.}\nWith CausalForest~\\cite{wager2018causal} ($K{=}4$, extreme, $n{=}500$), EC-HTE reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.191 to 0.039 (4.9$\\times$) with coverage 0.90; global amplifies bias up to 5.9$\\times$ (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}; bootstrap SE in Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_se}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Main results (extreme heterogeneity). Metrics averaged over subgroups, $z$-levels, 100 MC runs. Bold: lowest $|\\text{bias}|$. $^\\dagger$RMSE exceeds Global. $\\kappa \\leq 1.25$ guarantees strongest bias reduction; $\\kappa \\in (1.25, 3]$---covering all 13 tested LLM--dataset combinations---maintains calibrated coverage ($\\geq$93\\% vs.\\ Global 86--91\\%) with RMSE gains at $n_{\\text{expert}} \\geq 500$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_analysis}).\nBudget crossover curves, $K{=}8$ scalability, and multi-LLM validation ($R^2 = 0.975$) appear in Appendices~\\ref{sec:budget}--\\ref{app:multilm}.",

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Page 9 shows Limitations text continuing. The main text (through Conclusion) is entirely on pages 1-8. Everything looks good.

Let me now clean up the images and report to Director.

Let me verify one thing: the abstract word count. Let me count the updated abstract.

Updated abstract:
"Standard confusion-matrix correction for noisy annotations assumes uniform error rates. When this assumption fails, correction can increase rather than decrease bias in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) estimates, reversing the sign of estimated effect heterogeneity. LLMs, now widely used as proxy classifiers in computational social science, are the leading practical instance, with error rates varying by up to 5× across demographic subgroups. 

I'm deriving the exact bias decomposition for this scenario, showing how global correction introduces subgroup-level bias through a product of each subgroup's deviation from the pooled error rate, the treatment effect heterogeneity, and an amplification factor from matrix inversion. This decomposition reveals that correction can flip the sign of estimated conditional average treatment effects in 14.9% of configurations and across all eight real LLMs tested. The framework yields a diagnostic tool that predicts when correction becomes harmful using only per-subgroup error rates, achieving perfect discrimination on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity. The Error-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects approach addresses this by operating at the per-subgroup level rather than globally. The correction can flip the sign of estimated treatment effects in nearly 15% of realistic scenarios and across all eight real LLMs we tested. I've derived a diagnostic tool that predicts when this harmful correction occurs using only per-subgroup error rates, achieving perfect discrimination on heterogeneous subgroups. The per-subgroup correction approach I'm proposing achieves a 7.4× bias reduction under extreme heterogeneity with strong coverage guarantees through hierarchical Bayesian estimation, though this benefit depends on having sufficient error variation and adequate expert annotation budget.

Now I'm verifying that the key numbers stay consistent across the paper—the 14.9% figure for configurations appears uniformly in the abstract, introduction, figure captions, and conclusion, with the threshold examples at τ(0)=0.03 and τ(0)=0.01 also matching throughout.
```

  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch] 三个新实验结果需要整合进论文。DDL ~40h，这是最后一批新数据。\n\n**实验 1: Policy Reversal Zone τ Sweep（Excitement 核心）**\n结果数据：\n| τ(0) | Global reversal % | Naive reversal % |\n|------|-------------------|------------------|\n| 0.01 | 31.0% | 0% |\n| 0.02 | 21.6% | 0% |\n| 0.03 | 14.9% | 0% |\n| 0.05 | 6.1% | 0% |\n| 0.07 | 1.6% | 0% |\n| ≥0.10 | 0% | 0% |\n\nMost dangerous: τ(0)=0.01, δ_misclass=0.19 → 42.9% reversal。\nFigure: `artifacts/policy_reversal_zone.pdf`\n\n**整合方案**：在 Appendix 新建 section \"Policy Reversal Zone Analysis\"，引用 Figure。主文 §5 Experiments 加 1-2 句总结：\"When the true treatment effect is small (τ(0) < 0.05), global correction reverses the CATE sign in up to 31% of configurations (Figure X in Appendix).\"。这个 danger zone 结果也支撑 Intro 的 consequence framing。\n\n**实验 2: K=8 Scalability（消灭 SF7）**\n结果数据：\n| K | Method | |Bias| | RMSE | Coverage |\n|---|--------|-------|------|----------|\n| 2 | EC-HTE | 0.008 | 0.089 | 0.938 |\n| 4 | EC-HTE | 0.012 | 0.120 | 0.956 |\n| 8 | EC-HTE | 0.183 | 0.340 | 0.956 |\n| 2 | Global | 0.059 | 0.099 | 0.867 |\n| 4 | Global | 0.056 | 0.125 | 0.911 |\n| 8 | Global | 0.135 | 0.168 | 0.951 |\n\nK=8 下 EC-HTE 的 RMSE 爆炸（0.34 vs 0.17），根因是 250/8=31 labels/subgroup 太少。Coverage 仍 ≥95.6%。\n\n**整合方案**：Appendix 新建 \"K=8 Scalability Analysis\" table + 讨论。Limitations section 添加 1 句引用此结果。主文 §5 或 Conclusion 提一句 \"EC-HTE scales favorably to K=4 but requires expert budget to grow with K (Appendix Z.X).\"\n\n**实验 3: Treatment-Dependent Misclassification Sensitivity（消灭 SF3）**\n结果数据：\n| γ_T | EC-HTE Coverage | EC-HTE RMSE |\n|-----|----------------|-------------|\n| 0.00 | 0.945 | 0.075 |\n| 0.05 | 0.897 | 0.092 |\n| 0.10 | 0.750 | 0.133 |\n| 0.15 | 0.525 | 0.177 |\n| 0.20 | 0.370 | 0.224 |\n\nBreakeven at γ_T ≈ 0.10. EC-HTE robust to small violations (γ_T ≤ 0.05).\n\n**整合方案**：Appendix Z.2 扩展 (A1) sensitivity analysis，加入 treatment-dependent 场景 table + figure。主文 §5 加 1 句 \"EC-HTE maintains ≥90% coverage under modest treatment-dependent misclassification (γ_T ≤ 0.05; Appendix Z.2).\"\n\n**要求**：\n1. 三个实验全部写入 Appendix，主文各加 1-2 句引用\n2. 注意主文 ≤ 8 页限制（当前 0 余量），新增内容只放 appendix + 极简主文引用\n3. 图文件位于远程 local-cpu 的 `artifacts/` 目录，需要 Agent 拷贝到论文目录\n4. 这次不用验证引用"
}

**实验 1: Policy Reversal Zone τ Sweep（Excitement 核心）**
结果数据：
| τ(0) | Global reversal % | Naive reversal % |
|------|-------------------|------------------|
| 0.01 | 31.0% | 0% |
| 0.02 | 21.6% | 0% |
| 0.03 | 14.9% | 0% |
| 0.05 | 6.1% | 0% |
| 0.07 | 1.6% | 0% |
| ≥0.10 | 0% | 0% |

Most dangerous: τ(0)=0.01, δ_misclass=0.19 → 42.9% reversal。
Figure: `artifacts/policy_reversal_zone.pdf`

**整合方案**：在 Appendix 新建 section "Policy Reversal Zone Analysis"，引用 Figure。主文 §5 Experiments 加 1-2 句总结："When the true treatment effect is small (τ(0) < 0.05), global correction reverses the CATE sign in up to 31% of configurations (Figure X in Appendix)."。这个 danger zone 结果也支撑 Intro 的 consequence framing。

**实验 2: K=8 Scalability（消灭 SF7）**
结果数据：
| K | Method | |Bias| | RMSE | Coverage |
|---|--------|-------|------|----------|
| 2 | EC-HTE | 0.008 | 0.089 | 0.938 |
| 4 | EC-HTE | 0.012 | 0.120 | 0.956 |
| 8 | EC-HTE | 0.183 | 0.340 | 0.956 |
| 2 | Global | 0.059 | 0.099 | 0.867 |
| 4 | Global | 0.056 | 0.125 | 0.911 |
| 8 | Global | 0.135 | 0.168 | 0.951 |

K=8 下 EC-HTE 的 RMSE 爆炸（0.34 vs 0.17），根因是 250/8=31 labels/subgroup 太少。Coverage 仍 ≥95.6%。

**整合方案**：Appendix 新建 "K=8 Scalability Analysis" table + 讨论。Limitations section 添加 1 句引用此结果。主文 §5 或 Conclusion 提一句 "EC-HTE scales favorably to K=4 but requires expert budget to grow with K (Appendix Z.X)."

**实验 3: Treatment-Dependent Misclassification Sensitivity（消灭 SF3）**
结果数据：
| γ_T | EC-HTE Coverage | EC-HTE RMSE |
|-----|----------------|-------------|
| 0.00 | 0.945 | 0.075 |
| 0.05 | 0.897 | 0.092 |
| 0.10 | 0.750 | 0.133 |
| 0.15 | 0.525 | 0.177 |
| 0.20 | 0.370 | 0.224 |

Breakeven at γ_T ≈ 0.10. EC-HTE robust to small violations (γ_T ≤ 0.05).

**整合方案**：Appendix Z.2 扩展 (A1) sensitivity analysis，加入 treatment-dependent 场景 table + figure。主文 §5 加 1 句 "EC-HTE maintains ≥90% coverage under modest treatment-dependent misclassification (γ_T ≤ 0.05; Appendix Z.2)."

**要求**：
1. 三个实验全部写入 Appendix，主文各加 1-2 句引用
2. 注意主文 ≤ 8 页限制（当前 0 余量），新增内容只放 appendix + 极简主文引用
3. 图文件位于远程 local-cpu 的 `artifacts/` 目录，需要 Agent 拷贝到论文目录
4. 这次不用验证引用

```text
1	\section{EC-HTE Procedure}
2	\label{app:algorithm}
3	
4	\begin{algorithm}[t]
5	\caption{EC-HTE: Error-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects}
6	\label{alg:echte}
7	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
8	\Require Observations $\{(Y_i, W_i, \hat{Z}_i, X_i)\}_{i=1}^N$; expert labels $\{Z_j^*\}_{j=1}^{n_{\text{expert}}}$; subgroup map $S: \mathcal{X} \to \{1,\ldots,K\}$
9	\Ensure Corrected CATE estimates $\hat{\tau}(z)$ with variance-adjusted CIs
10	\Statex
11	\State \textbf{Preconditions:} verify $n_{\text{expert}} \times P(Z_{\text{minority}}) / K \geq 25$ and $\max_s(\text{FPR}_s, \text{FNR}_s) < 0.5$; if either fails, use global correction
12	\Statex
13	\State \textbf{Step 1: Estimate per-subgroup confusion matrices (HB)}
14	\For{$s = 1, \ldots, K$}
15	    \State $C_s[j,\cdot] \sim \mathrm{Dir}(\alpha_0 \cdot \hat{C}_{\text{global},j})$, \; $\alpha_0$ via empirical Bayes
16	    \State Gibbs sampler (4 chains, 4000 iter) on $\{(\hat{Z}_j, Z_j^*) : S_j = s\}$
17	    \State $\hat{C}_s \gets$ posterior mean; verify $\hat{R} < 1.01$
18	\EndFor
19	\Statex
20	\State \textbf{Step 2: Correct observed outcome moments}
21	\For{$s = 1, \ldots, K$}
22	    \If{$\kappa(\hat{C}_s) > 100$} $\hat{C}_s \gets \bar{C}$ \Comment{singularity guard}
23	    \EndIf
24	    \State $\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_s \gets \hat{C}_s^{-1} \hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_s$
25	\EndFor
26	\Statex
27	\State \textbf{Step 3: CausalForestDML with variance-adjusted SEs}
28	\State Train CausalForest (honest splitting) on corrected outcome moments $\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_s$
29	\State $\mathrm{se}_{\mathrm{adj},i} \gets \sqrt{\mathrm{diag}(\hat{C}_{S_i}^{-1}\, \mathrm{diag}(\mathrm{se}_i^2)\, \hat{C}_{S_i}^{-\top})}$
30	\State \Return $\hat{\tau}(z)$ with 95\% CIs from $\mathrm{se}_{\mathrm{adj}}$
31	\end{algorithmic}
32	\end{algorithm}
33	
34	\subsection{Condition Number Guard Ablation}
35	\label{app:kappa_ablation}
36	
37	The singularity guard in Algorithm~\ref{alg:echte} (line~6) falls back to global correction when $\kappa(\hat{C}_s) > 100$.
38	We verify that this threshold is not load-bearing by sweeping $\kappa \in \{10, 25, 50, 100, 200, 500, 1000\}$ under the standard DGP ($K{=}2$, extreme heterogeneity, $N{=}5{,}000$, $n_{\text{expert}}{=}250$, 100 MC runs).
39	Across all configurations, the maximum observed condition number is $\kappa_{\max} = 1.89$ (mean 1.45, p99 1.89), providing a $53{\times}$ safety margin below the $\kappa{=}100$ threshold.
40	Consequently, the guard never triggers at any tested threshold, and all $\kappa$ values produce identical RMSE (HB: 0.076, Global: 0.077) and zero numerical instability.
41	In a stress test with near-random labeling ($\pi_s \approx 0.4$--$0.5$, $n_{\text{expert}}{=}50$), the maximum condition number reaches 1{,}125, correctly triggering the $\kappa{=}100$ guard and preventing catastrophic inversion.
42	The adaptive formula $\kappa_{\text{adapt}} = 10 \sqrt{n_{\text{expert}} / K}$ recovers $\kappa{=}100$ for this regime and scales naturally with the estimation precision.
43	
44	\section{Full Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}}
45	\label{app:derivation}
46	
47	\paragraph{Setup.}
48	Under assumptions (A1)--(A4), the observed CATE in subgroup $s$ at effect-modifier level $z$ is
49	\begin{equation}
50	\tau_{\text{obs}}(z, s) = \sum_{z'} M_s[z, z'] \cdot \tau(z'),
51	\end{equation}
52	where $M_s[z, z'] = P(Z{=}z' \mid \hat{Z}{=}z, S{=}s)$ is the mixing matrix.
53	Under (A2)--(A3), $M_s = \bigl[\begin{smallmatrix} 1-\pi_s & \pi_s \\ \pi_s & 1-\pi_s \end{smallmatrix}\bigr]$.
54	
55	\paragraph{Global correction.}
56	The pooled mixing matrix is $M_{\text{global}} = \sum_s w_s M_s = \bigl[\begin{smallmatrix} 1-\bar{\pi} & \bar{\pi} \\ \bar{\pi} & 1-\bar{\pi} \end{smallmatrix}\bigr]$, with $\bar{\pi} = \sum_s w_s \pi_s$.
57	
58	Global correction applies $M_{\text{global}}^{-1}$ to $\tau_{\text{obs}}(\cdot, s)$:
59	\begin{equation}
60	\hat{\tau}_{\text{global}}(z, s) = [M_{\text{global}}^{-1} M_s \cdot \boldsymbol{\tau}]_z,
61	\end{equation}
62	where $\boldsymbol{\tau} = [\tau(0), \tau(1)]^\top$.
63	
64	\paragraph{Bias computation.}
65	The bias is $\hat{\tau}_{\text{global}}(z, s) - \tau(z) = [(M_{\text{global}}^{-1} M_s - I) \boldsymbol{\tau}]_z$.
66	
67	Direct computation gives:
68	\begin{equation}
69	M_{\text{global}}^{-1} = \frac{1}{1 - 2\bar{\pi}} \begin{bmatrix} 1-\bar{\pi} & -\bar{\pi} \\ -\bar{\pi} & 1-\bar{\pi} \end{bmatrix}.
70	\end{equation}
71	Multiplying by $M_s$:
72	\begin{equation}
73	\resizebox{.88\columnwidth}{!}{$\displaystyle
74	M_{\text{global}}^{-1} M_s = \frac{1}{1-2\bar{\pi}} \begin{bmatrix} (1{-}\bar{\pi})(1{-}\pi_s) - \bar{\pi}\pi_s & (1{-}\bar{\pi})\pi_s - \bar{\pi}(1{-}\pi_s) \\ -\bar{\pi}(1{-}\pi_s) + (1{-}\bar{\pi})\pi_s & -\bar{\pi}\pi_s + (1{-}\bar{\pi})(1{-}\pi_s) \end{bmatrix}.
75	$}
76	\end{equation}
77	
78	Define $\delta_s = \pi_s - \bar{\pi}$. Substituting $\pi_s = \bar{\pi} + \delta_s$:
79	\begin{equation}
80	M_{\text{global}}^{-1} M_s - I = \frac{\delta_s}{1-2\bar{\pi}} \begin{bmatrix} -1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{bmatrix}.
```

```text
340	\subsection{Scalability to $K{=}8$ Subgroups}
341	\label{app:k8_subgroups}
342	
343	We test EC-HTE at $K{=}8$ to identify the scalability boundary of hierarchical Bayesian partial pooling under fixed expert budget.
344	
345	\paragraph{Setup.}
346	$N{=}5{,}000$, $K{=}8$ (equal-weight subgroups), $n_{\text{expert}}{=}250$ (${\approx}31$ labels per subgroup), 100 MC replications.
347	Misclassification rates are uniformly spread: $\pi_s \in \{0.05, 0.09, 0.13, 0.18, 0.22, 0.26, 0.30, 0.35\}$.
348	Treatment effects depend on $Z$ (not subgroup): $\tau(Z{=}0){=}0$, $\tau(Z{=}1){=}0.3$.
349	
350	\begin{table}[h]
351	\centering
352	\caption{EC-HTE at $K{=}8$ ($n_{\text{expert}}{=}250$, ${\approx}31$ labels/subgroup, 100 MC).}
353	\label{tab:k8_subgroups}
354	\small
355	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
356	\toprule
357	Method & Coverage & RMSE & $|\text{Bias}|$ \\
358	\midrule
359	Oracle       & 0.944 & 0.113 & 0.090 \\
360	Global       & 0.951 & 0.168 & 0.135 \\
361	HB EC-HTE    & 0.956 & 0.340 & 0.183 \\
362	Strat MLE    & 0.953 & 0.638 & 0.219 \\
363	Naive        & 0.913 & 0.133 & 0.107 \\
364	\bottomrule
365	\end{tabular}
366	\end{table}
367	
368	All CM-aware methods achieve nominal coverage at $K{=}8$: HB EC-HTE 95.6\%, Strat MLE 95.3\%, Global 95.1\%.
369	HB partial pooling achieves a 47\% RMSE reduction over stratified MLE (0.340 vs.\ 0.638), confirming that hierarchical shrinkage provides substantial regularization.
370	However, HB RMSE (0.340) exceeds global correction (0.168), indicating that ${\approx}31$ expert labels per subgroup are insufficient for per-subgroup CM estimation to outweigh variance inflation.
371	
372	\paragraph{Per-subgroup breakdown.}
373	Table~\ref{tab:k8_per_subgroup} decomposes RMSE by subgroup.
374	
375	\begin{table}[h]
376	\centering
377	\caption{Per-subgroup RMSE at $K{=}8$. HB improvement over MLE is largest in high-$\pi$ subgroups but does not close the gap with Global.}
378	\label{tab:k8_per_subgroup}
379	\small
380	\begin{tabular}{cccccc}
381	\toprule
382	Subgroup & $\pi_s$ & HB RMSE & MLE RMSE & Global RMSE & HB Cov \\
383	\midrule
384	$s_0$ & 0.050 & 0.136 & 0.134 & \textbf{0.181} & 0.950 \\
385	$s_1$ & 0.093 & 0.133 & 0.132 & \textbf{0.154} & 0.970 \\
386	$s_2$ & 0.136 & 0.154 & 0.154 & 0.161 & 0.975 \\
387	$s_3$ & 0.179 & 0.178 & 0.182 & \textbf{0.159} & 0.945 \\
388	$s_4$ & 0.221 & 0.263 & 0.343 & \textbf{0.163} & 0.940 \\
389	$s_5$ & 0.264 & 0.421 & 0.711 & \textbf{0.164} & 0.965 \\
390	$s_6$ & 0.307 & 0.634 & 1.375 & \textbf{0.177} & 0.940 \\
391	$s_7$ & 0.350 & 0.432 & 0.809 & \textbf{0.183} & 0.960 \\
392	\bottomrule
393	\end{tabular}
394	\end{table}
395	
396	RMSE degradation is concentrated in high-$\pi$ subgroups ($s_4$--$s_7$, $\pi \geq 0.22$), where confusion matrix inversion amplifies estimation noise ($r = 0.87$, $p = 0.005$ for correlation between $\pi_s$ and HB RMSE).
397	Low-error subgroups ($s_0$--$s_1$) benefit from stratification, with HB RMSE 14--25\% below global correction.
398	HB partial pooling reduces MLE RMSE by 24--54\% in the high-$\pi$ regime ($s_4$--$s_7$), confirming that hierarchical shrinkage provides meaningful regularization even when per-subgroup labels are scarce.
399	Coverage remains $\geq$0.94 across all subgroups for HB EC-HTE, indicating that variance inflation does not compromise inference validity.
400	Adaptive shrinkage---increasing the prior strength for high-$\pi$ subgroups where CM estimation is least reliable---is a natural extension that may close the RMSE gap with global correction at larger $K$.
401	
402	We explored adaptive shrinkage ($\alpha{=}10$ for high-$\pi$ subgroups vs.\ default $\alpha{=}3$), which reduces aggregate RMSE from 0.340 to 0.216 (35\% improvement) by attenuating estimation noise in high-$\pi$ subgroups.
403	However, RMSE remains 30\% above global correction (0.166), confirming that the bottleneck is per-subgroup sample size (${\sim}$31 expert labels) rather than the shrinkage method itself.
404	This establishes $K{=}8$ at $n_{\text{expert}}{=}250$ as a boundary condition: all correction methods provide valid inference (coverage $\geq$94\%), but per-subgroup stratification requires proportionally larger expert budgets---approximately $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 500$ (${\approx}60$ labels/subgroup)---to achieve RMSE parity with global correction.
405	
406	\section{Asymmetric Misclassification Extension}
407	\label{app:asymmetric}
408	
409	Assumption~(A2) imposes symmetric misclassification ($\text{FPR}_s = \text{FNR}_s = \pi_s$), which simplifies the bias decomposition but is not necessary for it.

1244	\subsection{Assumption (A1): Sensitivity to Outcome-Dependent Misclassification}
1245	\label{app:a1_sensitivity}
1246	
1247	We assess EC-HTE's robustness when misclassification depends on potential outcomes, violating~(A1).
1248	We modify the synthetic DGP ($K{=}2$, extreme heterogeneity, $N{=}5{,}000$, $n_{\text{expert}}{=}250$) so that misclassification probability becomes outcome-dependent: $P(\hat{Z}{\neq}Z \mid Z, S, Y) = \mathrm{clamp}(\pi_s + \gamma \cdot (Y - \mu_Y)/\sigma_Y,\, 0,\, 1)$, where $\gamma \in \{0, 0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.20\}$ controls the strength of violation.
1249	Table~\ref{tab:a1_sensitivity} reports results averaged over 100 MC runs.
1250	
1251	\begin{table}[h]
1252	\centering
1253	\caption{Sensitivity to Assumption~(A1) violation. Coverage and $|\text{bias}|$ under outcome-dependent misclassification ($\gamma > 0$).}
1254	\label{tab:a1_sensitivity}
1255	\small
1256	\begin{tabular}{lccc ccc}
1257	\toprule
1258	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{Coverage (\%)} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{$|\text{Bias}|$} \\
1259	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-7}
1260	$\gamma$ & EC-HTE & Global & Naive & EC-HTE & Global & Naive \\
1261	\midrule
1262	0.00 & 95.5 & 92.7 & 87.7 & 0.060 & 0.065 & 0.061 \\
1263	0.05 & 95.5 & 93.5 & 83.8 & 0.059 & 0.064 & 0.064 \\
1264	0.10 & 95.3 & 93.8 & 83.5 & 0.063 & 0.064 & 0.067 \\
1265	0.15 & 94.8 & 94.3 & 79.2 & 0.065 & 0.064 & 0.070 \\
1266	0.20 & 93.5 & 94.0 & 79.5 & 0.068 & 0.063 & 0.073 \\
1267	\bottomrule
1268	\end{tabular}
1269	\end{table}
1270	
1271	EC-HTE maintains near-nominal coverage ($\geq$93.5\%) across the full $\gamma$ range, with $|\text{bias}|$ increasing only 13.7\% from $\gamma{=}0$ to $\gamma{=}0.2$.
1272	All correction methods (Global, Stratified MLE, HB) maintain $\geq$90\% coverage, indicating that moderate (A1) violations do not catastrophically degrade confusion-matrix-based correction.
1273	The naive estimator degrades most rapidly (coverage $-$8.2pp), consistent with outcome-dependent misclassification compounding the uncorrected label noise.
1274	
1275	\subsection{Extension to Confounded Treatment Assignment}
1276	\label{app:confounded_dgp}
1277	
1278	EC-HTE's confusion matrix correction operates on estimated CATEs regardless of how those estimates are produced.

1395	\end{tabular}
1396	\end{table}
1397	
1398	EC-HTE with the asymmetric formula achieves 94.5\% coverage versus 93.0\% under the symmetric assumption ($+1.5$pp), with RMSE improved from 0.083 to 0.074 ($-11.5\%$).
1399	The improvement concentrates in the high-error subgroup ($s_1$): asymmetric coverage is 93.0\% vs.\ symmetric 91.0\%.
1400	Practitioners should estimate full $2 \times 2$ confusion matrices rather than assuming symmetric misclassification, particularly when FPR and FNR differ substantially.
1401	
1402	\subsection{Complementarity with LLM Calibration}
1403	\label{app:calibration}
1404	
1405	Temperature or Platt scaling~\cite{guo2017calibration} can reduce per-subgroup misclassification rates, but does not eliminate heterogeneity $\Delta\pi$.
1406	We simulate calibration by shrinking per-subgroup rates from $\pi{=}[0.05, 0.25]$ ($\Delta\pi{=}0.20$) to $\pi{=}[0.08, 0.15]$ ($\Delta\pi{=}0.07$), testing whether EC-HTE remains necessary post-calibration.
1407	Setup: $K{=}2$, $N{=}5{,}000$, $n_{\text{expert}}{=}250$, 100 MC runs.
1408	
1409	\begin{table}[h]
1410	\centering
1411	\caption{Calibration reduces $\Delta\pi$ but does not eliminate it; EC-HTE corrects the residual bias. Best coverage is achieved by combining both.}
1412	\label{tab:calibration}
1413	\small
1414	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
1415	\toprule
1416	Method & $|\text{Bias}|$ & RMSE & Coverage \\
1417	\midrule
1418	Oracle                    & 0.043 & 0.053 & 0.963 \\
1419	Calibrated + EC-HTE       & 0.055 & 0.069 & \textbf{0.965} \\
1420	Calibrated + Global       & 0.055 & 0.068 & 0.963 \\
1421	Calibrated + Naive        & 0.051 & 0.064 & 0.913 \\
1422	Uncalibrated + EC-HTE     & 0.064 & 0.084 & 0.938 \\
1423	Uncalibrated + Global     & 0.069 & 0.085 & 0.920 \\
1424	Uncalibrated + Naive      & 0.063 & 0.079 & 0.840 \\
1425	\bottomrule
1426	\end{tabular}
1427	\end{table}
1428	
1429	Calibration alone improves naive coverage from 84.0\% to 91.3\% but does not reach the nominal 95\%.
1430	Calibrated + EC-HTE achieves the highest coverage (96.5\%), demonstrating that calibration and EC-HTE address complementary error sources: calibration reduces per-subgroup $\pi_s$ while EC-HTE corrects the residual $\delta_s$-driven bias from heterogeneity that persists post-calibration.
1431	Propensity estimation robustness under model misspecification is a complementary concern addressed by the IPW literature; EC-HTE as a downstream correction layer does not alter IPW behavior.
1432

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UVMF71pGF9Lg9pfUF9QFx1`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	EC-HTE requires $n_{\text{expert}} \times P(Z_{\text{minority}}) / K \geq 25$ per subgroup for stable hierarchical Bayesian estimation.
5	For $K{=}2$, EC-HTE remains effective even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$ (Appendix~\ref{app:classimbalance}); for $K{=}4$, $P(Z{=}1) \geq 0.3$ is needed to approach breakeven with global correction at $n_{\text{expert}}{=}250$.
6	Even above this threshold, stratification incurs a variance cost: at $K{=}4$, extreme heterogeneity, and $n_{\text{expert}} \leq 100$, CausalForest RMSE reaches 3.5$\times$ that of the naive estimator.
7	The method also requires moderate baseline classifier accuracy per subgroup; when any subgroup's FNR or FPR approaches 0.5, the confusion matrix row approaches a uniform distribution and inversion amplifies noise (Appendix~\ref{app:semireal}), as confirmed independently in the hate speech case study (Appendix~\ref{app:hatespeech}).
8	These failure modes interact: high per-subgroup error-rate variance combined with low minority prevalence $P(Z{=}1)$ jointly reduces effective per-subgroup expert labels for the positive class, compounding both sources of estimation error.
9	Additionally, our main semi-synthetic experiments use constant treatment effects across subgroups; a supplementary heterogeneous-$\tau$ DGP (Appendix~\ref{app:multilm_opensource}) shows that EC-HTE achieves targeted bias and RMSE improvement in subgroups with large $\Delta\tau$, though aggregate RMSE remains comparable to global correction.
10	At $K{=}8$ with $n_{\text{expert}}{=}250$ (${\approx}31$ labels/subgroup), EC-HTE maintains nominal coverage (95.6\%) but RMSE exceeds global correction, establishing $K{=}8$ as a scalability boundary under fixed budget (Appendix~\ref{app:k8_subgroups}).
11	The hierarchical prior implicitly performs soft merging---subgroups with insufficient data are shrunk toward the global estimate---but explicit hierarchical subgroup merging or adaptive budget allocation may extend the applicability frontier beyond $K{=}8$; we leave these extensions to future work.
12	
13	Assumption~(A1) (conditional independence of misclassification and potential outcomes) is not empirically testable.
14	When LLM accuracy correlates with outcome magnitude---e.g., extreme sentiment is both easier to classify and associated with larger treatment effects---Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} acquires an $O(\gamma)$ perturbation (Section~\ref{sec:theory}).
15	Sensitivity simulation (Appendix~\ref{app:a1_sensitivity}) shows that EC-HTE maintains near-nominal coverage ($\geq$93.5\%) under outcome-dependent misclassification up to $\gamma = 0.2$, with bias increasing only 13.7\%; developing a formal closed-form bound for the $O(\gamma)$ perturbation remains a natural extension~\cite{lash2009applying,dorn2022sharp}.
16	Treatment-dependent misclassification ($P(\hat{Z} \mid Z, T) \neq P(\hat{Z} \mid Z)$), where LLM accuracy varies with treatment status, violates (A1) analogously; the $O(\gamma)$ perturbation structure applies symmetrically, and sensitivity analysis for this case is left to future work.
17	
18	Oracle experiments confirm CausalForest plug-in SE is well-calibrated (94--96\% coverage with true $Z$ labels); EC-HTE plug-in SE achieves 93.8\% aggregate coverage under standard hyperparameters ($n_{\text{estimators}}{=}200$), with residual under-coverage concentrated in high-error subgroups due to asymmetric $M^{-1}$ SE propagation.
19	A full bootstrap procedure ($B{=}100$; re-estimate CM and re-fit per replicate) recovers $\geq$95\% coverage across all expert budgets tested ($n_{\text{expert}} \in \{25, 50, 75, 100, 200\}$; Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_se}); even plug-in SE achieves $\geq$91\% at $n_{\text{expert}}{=}25$.
20	For the DIM estimator, plug-in SE achieves $\geq$95\% coverage across all budgets.
21	
22	The diagnostic (Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}) achieves AUC $= 0.86$ under synthetic symmetric errors but degrades to $0.63$ on real LLM confusion matrices (pooled across 16 model--dataset combinations; Appendix~\ref{app:screening}).
23	For $K{=}2$, the diagnostic anti-predicts because $|\delta_0|{=}|\delta_1|$ forces score differentiation onto $1/\pi_s$ alone, which inversely correlates with actual harm; for binary $Z$, the asymmetric $B_s$-based formulation (Remark~2) is algebraically equivalent and offers no improvement.
24	Performance is strongest for $K{\geq}4$ with moderate asymmetry (AUC $= 0.66$; Appendix~\ref{app:asymmetric}).
25	The diagnostic remains a theoretically grounded criterion---the bias identity's product structure ensures it correctly identifies the \emph{direction} of bias amplification---but should be paired with $R^2$ validation of the bias identity rather than used as a standalone screening rule.
26	
27	All main experiments use symmetric error rates and additive CATE injection ($Y(1) = Y(0) + \tau(Z)$).
28	The bias identity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}) generalizes to asymmetric confusion matrices (Appendix~\ref{app:asymmetric}, $R^2 = 0.991$) and derives from the confusion matrix structure rather than the outcome model.
29	While our main experiments use additive CATE injection, a supplementary experiment with multiplicative effects ($Y(1) = Y(0) \times \exp(\tau(Z))$) yields comparable bias reduction (52\%) and nominal coverage (0.955; Appendix~\ref{app:rlearner}), confirming robustness to the functional form of treatment effects.
30	EC-HTE further requires discrete, pre-defined subgroups; subgroup definitions should be pre-specified based on domain knowledge, as data-driven subgroup discovery risks overfitting the confusion matrix structure to sample-specific noise. Continuous effect modifiers or latent subgroup discovery settings require different approaches; discretizing a continuous modifier introduces grouping bias whose magnitude depends on bin width, and intersectional subgroups with many levels may exceed the $n_{\text{expert}} \times P(Z_{\text{minority}}) / K \geq 25$ budget threshold.
31	Our experiments define subgroups by text features (hashtag presence, text length); demographic subgroups---where prior work documents the most concerning disparities \citep{ashwin2025bias}---require sensitive data access beyond the scope of this study.
32	EC-HTE's correction mechanism is agnostic to subgroup definition: it corrects any per-subgroup confusion-matrix heterogeneity, whether defined by text features, demographics, or other covariates.
33	Ground-truth subgroup CATEs are unavailable in public datasets; classic causal inference benchmarks (e.g., LaLonde, IHDP) lack the LLM annotation step that creates the measurement error our method corrects, precluding direct application. We use synthetic CATE injection on real text features and real LLM annotations.
34	We leave full real-data validation---on a dataset combining RCT design, ground-truth CATEs, and LLM annotations---to future work as suitable public benchmarks become available.
35	Code and data generation scripts will be released upon acceptance.
```

      "content": "\nEC-HTE maintains near-nominal coverage ($\\geq$93.5\\%) across the full $\\gamma$ range, with $|\\text{bias}|$ increasing only 13.7\\% from $\\gamma{=}0$ to $\\gamma{=}0.2$.\nAll correction methods (Global, Stratified MLE, HB) maintain $\\geq$90\\% coverage, indicating that moderate (A1) violations do not catastrophically degrade confusion-matrix-based correction.\nThe naive estimator degrades most rapidly (coverage $-$8.2pp), consistent with outcome-dependent misclassification compounding the uncorrected label noise.\n\n\\subsection{Extension to Confounded Treatment Assignment}\n\\label{app:confounded_dgp}\n\nEC-HTE's confusion matrix correction operates on estimated CATEs regardless of how those estimates are produced.\nTo verify compatibility with observational (non-RCT) designs, we pair EC-HTE with an inverse probability weighted difference-in-means (IPW-DIM) estimator under confounded treatment assignment.",

  "old_string": "The naive estimator degrades most rapidly (coverage $-$8.2pp), consistent with outcome-dependent misclassification compounding the uncorrected label noise.\n\n\\subsection{Extension to Confounded Treatment Assignment}",
  "new_string": "The naive estimator degrades most rapidly (coverage $-$8.2pp), consistent with outcome-dependent misclassification compounding the uncorrected label noise.\n\n\\subsection{Assumption (A1): Treatment-Dependent Misclassification}\n\\label{app:treatment_dep}\n\nWe extend the (A1) sensitivity analysis to the treatment-dependent case, where misclassification probability depends on treatment assignment $T$: $P(\\hat{Z}{\\neq}Z \\mid Z, S, T) = \\mathrm{clamp}(\\pi_s + \\gamma_T \\cdot (2T - 1),\\, 0,\\, 1)$, with $\\gamma_T \\in \\{0, 0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.20\\}$.\nThis scenario arises when LLM accuracy varies with treatment status---e.g., treated texts may be systematically harder to classify.\nSetup: $K{=}2$, extreme heterogeneity, $N{=}5{,}000$, $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}250$, 100 MC runs.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Sensitivity to treatment-dependent misclassification ($\\gamma_T$). EC-HTE maintains $\\geq$89.7\\% coverage at $\\gamma_T \\leq 0.05$.}\n\\label{tab:treatment_dep}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n$\\gamma_T$ & EC-HTE Coverage & EC-HTE RMSE \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.00 & 0.945 & 0.075 \\\\\n0.05 & 0.897 & 0.092 \\\\\n0.10 & 0.750 & 0.133 \\\\\n0.15 & 0.525 & 0.177 \\\\\n0.20 & 0.370 & 0.224 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nEC-HTE is robust to small treatment-dependent violations ($\\gamma_T \\leq 0.05$), maintaining $\\geq$89.7\\% coverage with modest RMSE increase (0.075 to 0.092).\nAt $\\gamma_T = 0.10$, coverage drops to 75.0\\%, establishing this as the approximate breakeven point.\nThe degradation is steeper than for outcome-dependent violations (Table~\\ref{tab:a1_sensitivity}), because treatment-dependent errors directly contaminate the treatment arm contrast.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:treatment_dep} visualizes the coverage and RMSE trajectories.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/treatment_dep_misclass.pdf}\n\\caption{EC-HTE coverage and RMSE under treatment-dependent misclassification. Dashed line: 90\\% coverage threshold. Breakeven at $\\gamma_T \\approx 0.10$.}\n\\label{fig:treatment_dep}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Extension to Confounded Treatment Assignment}"
}

  "new_string": "Propensity estimation robustness under model misspecification is a complementary concern addressed by the IPW literature; EC-HTE as a downstream correction layer does not alter IPW behavior.\n\n\\section{Policy Reversal Zone Analysis}\n\\label{app:policy_reversal}\n\nWhen the true treatment effect is small, global correction can reverse the sign of estimated CATE---concluding a beneficial treatment is harmful---creating a \\emph{policy reversal zone}.\nWe sweep $\\tau(0) \\in \\{0.01, 0.02, 0.03, 0.05, 0.07, 0.10\\}$ under the standard DGP ($K{=}2$, extreme heterogeneity, $N{=}5{,}000$, $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}250$, 100 MC runs; $\\Delta\\tau{=}0.30$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Policy reversal zone: fraction of configurations where global correction reverses the sign of estimated CATE.\nThe naive estimator produces zero sign reversals across all $\\tau(0)$ values; EC-HTE also achieves 0\\% reversal.}\n\\label{tab:policy_reversal}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{ccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\tau(0)$ & Global reversal \\% & Most extreme $\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{global}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.01 & 31.0 & $-0.079$ \\\\\n0.02 & 21.6 & --- \\\\\n0.03 & 14.9 & $-0.059$ \\\\\n0.05 & 6.1  & $-0.039$ \\\\\n0.07 & 1.6  & --- \\\\\n$\\geq$0.10 & 0 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe reversal rate increases monotonically as $\\tau(0)$ decreases: at $\\tau(0) = 0.01$, 31\\% of configurations yield sign reversal, with the most extreme case estimating $\\hat{\\tau} = -0.079$ when the true effect is $\\tau = +0.01$.\nThe critical threshold is $\\tau(0) < 0.089$, matching the closed-form prediction from Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}: $|\\delta_{\\max} \\cdot \\Delta\\tau_{\\max}| / (1 - 2\\bar{\\pi}) = 0.125 \\times 0.50 / 0.70 = 0.089$.\nThe most dangerous combination ($\\tau(0) = 0.01$, $\\delta_{\\text{misclass}} = 0.19$) reaches 42.9\\% reversal rate.\nThe naive estimator produces zero sign reversals across all $\\tau(0)$ values, confirming that reversal is an artifact of global correction, not inherent estimation noise.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:policy_reversal} visualizes the reversal zone across the $\\Delta\\pi \\times \\tau(0)$ parameter space.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/policy_reversal_zone.pdf}\n\\caption{Policy reversal zone: sign-reversal rate as a function of $\\tau(0)$ and $\\Delta\\pi$. Shaded region marks configurations where global correction reverses the CATE sign, directing policy toward the wrong subgroups.}\n\\label{fig:policy_reversal}\n\\end{figure}"
}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01SEWKXJd9Vbe3cSyoVbSNcG`</summary>

```text
0	\section{Experiments}
1	
2	\subsection{Setup}
3	
4	We test on synthetic ($N{=}5{,}000$, $K \in \{2, 4\}$, controlled $\Delta\tau$ and $\Delta\pi$, 100 MC runs) and semi-synthetic (TweetEval sentiment~\cite{barbieri2020tweeteval}, $K{=}4$ unbalanced subgroups, $\pi \in \{0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.25\}$) DGPs; full specifications in Appendix~\ref{app:dgp}.
5	
6	We compare: (1) \textit{Naive} (no correction), (2) \textit{Global} (pooled confusion matrix, equivalent to PPI/DSL~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,egami2024dsl} with homogeneous error), (3) \textit{Stratified MLE} (per-subgroup DSL without shrinkage), (4) \textit{HB EC-HTE} (our method), (5) \textit{PPCI}~\cite{sui2026ppci}, and (6) \textit{EC-HTE+PPCI}.
7	For CausalForest~\cite{wager2018causal}, we compare naive, global, and EC-HTE variants.
8	Primary metrics are $|\text{bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\% CI coverage, averaged across subgroups and 100 MC runs.
9	Robustness validation covers hate speech, six OpenAI models, subgroup misspecification, asymmetric confusion matrices, confounded treatment, and outcome-dependent misclassification (all $\geq$93.5\% coverage; Appendices~\ref{app:hatespeech}--\ref{app:a1_sensitivity}).
10	
11	\subsection{Empirical Validation of the Bias Identity}
12	\label{sec:bias_mechanism}
13	
14	We verify Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} on a $K{=}2$ grid (520 configurations, $\Delta\pi \in [0, 0.25]$, $\Delta\tau \in (0, 0.50]$) and a $K{=}4$ grid (Appendix~\ref{app:dgp}).
15	Regressing observed subgroup bias on $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau / (1{-}2\bar{\pi})$ yields $R^2 = 0.994$ ($K{=}2$) and $0.974$ ($K{=}4$); residual variance reflects MC noise ($R^2 \geq 0.976$ even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$; Appendix~\ref{app:classimbalance}).
16	Global correction worsens bias in 50\% of $K{=}2$ configurations (260/520, excluding $\Delta\pi{=}0$), with amplification reaching 2.86$\times$ ($K{=}2$) and 2.41$\times$ ($K{=}4$); Figure~\ref{fig:heatmap} visualizes the amplification surface.
17	Beyond amplification, global correction reverses the sign of estimated CATE---concluding a protective treatment is harmful---in 14.9\% of configurations at $\tau(0) = 0.03$ (31\% at $\tau(0) = 0.01$; critical threshold $\tau(0) < 0.089$, matching Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}), with all eight real LLMs exhibiting the same pattern; EC-HTE eliminates sign reversal entirely (Figure~\ref{fig:sign_reversal}).
18	
19	\begin{figure*}[t]
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe test on synthetic ($N{=}5{,}000$, $K \\in \\{2, 4\\}$, controlled $\\Delta\\tau$ and $\\Delta\\pi$, 100 MC runs) and semi-synthetic (TweetEval sentiment~\\cite{barbieri2020tweeteval}, $K{=}4$ unbalanced subgroups, $\\pi \\in \\{0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.25\\}$) DGPs; full specifications in Appendix~\\ref{app:dgp}.\n\nWe compare: (1) \\textit{Naive} (no correction), (2) \\textit{Global} (pooled confusion matrix, equivalent to PPI/DSL~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,egami2024dsl} with homogeneous error), (3) \\textit{Stratified MLE} (per-subgroup DSL without shrinkage), (4) \\textit{HB EC-HTE} (our method), (5) \\textit{PPCI}~\\cite{sui2026ppci}, and (6) \\textit{EC-HTE+PPCI}.\nFor CausalForest~\\cite{wager2018causal}, we compare naive, global, and EC-HTE variants.\nPrimary metrics are $|\\text{bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\\% CI coverage, averaged across subgroups and 100 MC runs.\nRobustness validation covers hate speech, six OpenAI models, subgroup misspecification, asymmetric confusion matrices, confounded treatment, and outcome-dependent misclassification (all $\\geq$93.5\\% coverage; Appendices~\\ref{app:hatespeech}--\\ref{app:a1_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation of the Bias Identity}\n\\label{sec:bias_mechanism}\n\nWe verify Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity} on a $K{=}2$ grid (520 configurations, $\\Delta\\pi \\in [0, 0.25]$, $\\Delta\\tau \\in (0, 0.50]$) and a $K{=}4$ grid (Appendix~\\ref{app:dgp}).\nRegressing observed subgroup bias on $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau / (1{-}2\\bar{\\pi})$ yields $R^2 = 0.994$ ($K{=}2$) and $0.974$ ($K{=}4$); residual variance reflects MC noise ($R^2 \\geq 0.976$ even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$; Appendix~\\ref{app:classimbalance}).\nGlobal correction worsens bias in 50\\% of $K{=}2$ configurations (260/520, excluding $\\Delta\\pi{=}0$), with amplification reaching 2.86$\\times$ ($K{=}2$) and 2.41$\\times$ ($K{=}4$); Figure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} visualizes the amplification surface.\nBeyond amplification, global correction reverses the sign of estimated CATE---concluding a protective treatment is harmful---in 14.9\\% of configurations at $\\tau(0) = 0.03$ (31\\% at $\\tau(0) = 0.01$; critical threshold $\\tau(0) < 0.089$, matching Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}), with all eight real LLMs exhibiting the same pattern; EC-HTE eliminates sign reversal entirely (Figure~\\ref{fig:sign_reversal}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig2_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Bias amplification under global correction ($K{=}2$). (a) Low-error subgroup: correction always helps. (b) High-error subgroup: correction \\emph{increases} bias when $\\Delta\\pi > 0.12$ (hatched), reaching 2.86$\\times$.}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{EC-HTE Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:ec_hte_eval}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} presents the main results across representative configurations.\nIn the $K{=}2$ extreme row, global correction \\emph{increases} $|\\text{bias}|$ relative to naive (0.059 vs.\\ 0.058), confirming the over-correction mechanism of Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}.\nF-L scalar correction ($\\hat{\\tau}/(1{-}2\\bar{\\pi})$) increases $|\\text{bias}|$ to 0.122 with coverage 0.68---worse than naive---because a single scalar cannot correct heterogeneous attenuation; F-L per-subgroup inflates bias to 0.168 as $(1{-}2\\pi_s) \\to 0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).\nHB EC-HTE reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.008 (7.4$\\times$) at $K{=}2$ and from 0.056 to 0.012 (4.7$\\times$) at $K{=}4$, maintaining near-nominal coverage (0.94--0.96) with lower RMSE than stratified MLE.\nUnder moderate heterogeneity and in TweetEval ($K{=}4$), methods converge ($p > 0.05$); EC-HTE incurs higher RMSE when $\\Delta\\pi$ is small (Table~\\ref{tab:main_supp}).\n\n\\paragraph{CausalForest.}\nWith CausalForest~\\cite{wager2018causal} ($K{=}4$, extreme, $n{=}500$), EC-HTE reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.191 to 0.039 (4.9$\\times$) with coverage 0.90; global amplifies bias up to 5.9$\\times$ (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}; bootstrap SE in Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_se}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Main results (extreme heterogeneity). Metrics averaged over subgroups, $z$-levels, 100 MC runs. Bold: lowest $|\\text{bias}|$. $^\\dagger$RMSE exceeds Global. F-L Scalar: $\\hat{\\tau}/(1{-}2\\bar{\\pi})$~\\cite{frazis2003estimating}; F-L Per-sub.: per-subgroup variant. Moderate heterogeneity and TweetEval results in Table~\\ref{tab:main_supp}; MC SEs in Appendix~\\ref{app:mcse}.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{ll ccc}\n\\toprule\nConfiguration & Method & $|\\text{Bias}|$ & RMSE & 95\\% CI Cov. \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{6}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{DIM\\\\$K{=}2$, $n{=}250$}}\n & Naive & 0.058 & 0.090 & 0.82 \\\\\n & Global & 0.059 & 0.099 & 0.87 \\\\\n & F-L Scalar & 0.122 & 0.159 & 0.68 \\\\\n & F-L Per-sub. & 0.168 & 0.220 & 0.65 \\\\\n & Strat.\\ MLE & 0.012 & 0.094 & 0.95 \\\\\n & HB EC-HTE & \\textbf{0.008} & 0.089 & 0.94 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{DIM\\\\$K{=}4$, $n{=}500$}}\n & Naive & 0.062 & 0.113 & 0.88 \\\\\n & Global & 0.056 & 0.125 & 0.91 \\\\\n & Strat.\\ MLE & \\textbf{0.010} & 0.127$^\\dagger$ & 0.95 \\\\\n & HB EC-HTE & 0.012 & 0.120 & 0.96 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{CausalForest\\\\$K{=}4$, $n{=}500$}}\n & Naive & 0.208 & 0.231 & 0.44 \\\\\n & Global & 0.191 & 0.244 & 0.58 \\\\\n & EC-HTE Forest & \\textbf{0.039} & 0.180 & 0.90 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Actionable Diagnostic Without EC-HTE}\n\\label{sec:diagnostic}\n\nProposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts} provides a $\\Delta\\tau$-free diagnostic: global correction increases bias in subgroup $s$ iff $|\\delta_s| / ((1-2\\bar{\\pi}) \\cdot \\pi_s) > 1$, computable from a pilot sample without estimating treatment effects.\nA threshold classifier achieves AUC = 0.86 at the subgroup level (Figure~\\ref{fig:roc}, Appendix).\nOn real LLM confusion matrices (16 combinations; Appendix~\\ref{app:screening}), AUC drops to 0.63; restricting to subgroups with $\\tau_s > 0$ recovers AUC $= 1.0$.\nFor $K{=}2$, $|\\delta_0|{=}|\\delta_1|$ limits discrimination; a meta-criterion ($\\max_s \\mathrm{score}(s) > 1$) recovers reliability (AUC $\\geq 0.75$) for $K{\\geq}4$, while $\\max_s \\mathrm{score}(s) \\leq 1$ signals unreliable regimes (Appendix~\\ref{app:screening}).\n\nThe condition number $\\kappa(\\hat{C}_s)$ governs correction at three levels: $\\kappa > 100$ triggers the singularity guard; $\\kappa \\leq 1.25$ guarantees strongest bias reduction; $\\kappa \\in (1.25, 3]$---covering all 13 tested LLM--dataset combinations---maintains calibrated coverage ($\\geq$93\\% vs.\\ Global 86--91\\%) with RMSE gains at $n_{\\text{expert}} \\geq 500$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_analysis}).\nBudget crossover curves, $K{=}8$ scalability, and multi-LLM validation ($R^2 = 0.975$) appear in Appendices~\\ref{sec:budget}--\\ref{app:multilm}.",

(b) High-error subgroup: correction \\emph{increases} bias when $\\Delta\\pi > 0.12$ (hatched), reaching 2.86$\\times$.}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{EC-HTE Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:ec_hte_eval}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} presents the main results across representative configurations.\nIn the $K{=}2$ extreme row, global correction \\emph{increases} $|\\text{bias}|$ relative to naive (0.059 vs.\\ 0.058), confirming the over-correction mechanism of Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}.\nF-L scalar correction ($\\hat{\\tau}/(1{-}2\\bar{\\pi})$) increases $|\\text{bias}|$ to 0.122 with coverage 0.68---worse than naive---because a single scalar cannot correct heterogeneous attenuation; F-L per-subgroup inflates bias to 0.168 as $(1{-}2\\pi_s) \\to 0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).\nHB EC-HTE reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.008 (7.4$\\times$) at $K{=}2$ and from 0.056 to 0.012 (4.7$\\times$) at $K{=}4$, maintaining near-nominal coverage (0.94--0.96) with lower RMSE than stratified MLE.\nUnder moderate heterogeneity and in TweetEval ($K{=}4$), methods converge ($p > 0.05$); EC-HTE incurs higher RMSE when $\\Delta\\pi$ is small (Table~\\ref{tab:main_supp}).\n\n\\paragraph{CausalForest.}\nWith CausalForest~\\cite{wager2018causal} ($K{=}4$, extreme, $n{=}500$), EC-HTE reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.191 to 0.039 (4.9$\\times$) with coverage 0.90; global amplifies bias up to 5.9$\\times$ (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}; bootstrap SE in Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_se}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Main results (extreme heterogeneity). Metrics averaged over subgroups, $z$-levels, 100 MC runs. Bold: lowest $|\\text{bias}|$. $^\\dagger$RMSE exceeds Global. F-L Scalar: $\\hat{\\tau}/(1{-}2\\bar{\\pi})$~\\cite{frazis2003estimating}; F-L Per-sub.: per-subgroup variant. Moderate heterogeneity and TweetEval results in Table~\\ref{tab:main_supp}; MC SEs in Appendix~\\ref{app:mcse}.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{ll ccc}\n\\toprule\nConfiguration & Method & $|\\text{Bias}|$ & RMSE & 95\\% CI Cov. \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{6}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{DIM\\\\$K{=}2$, $n{=}250$}}\n & Naive & 0.058 & 0.090 & 0.82 \\\\\n & Global & 0.059 & 0.099 & 0.87 \\\\\n & F-L Scalar & 0.122 & 0.159 & 0.68 \\\\\n & F-L Per-sub. & 0.168 & 0.220 & 0.65 \\\\\n & Strat.\\ MLE & 0.012 & 0.094 & 0.95 \\\\\n & HB EC-HTE & \\textbf{0.008} & 0.089 & 0.94 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{DIM\\\\$K{=}4$, $n{=}500$}}\n & Naive & 0.062 & 0.113 & 0.88 \\\\\n & Global & 0.056 & 0.125 & 0.91 \\\\\n & Strat.\\ MLE & \\textbf{0.010} & 0.127$^\\dagger$ & 0.95 \\\\\n & HB EC-HTE & 0.012 & 0.120 & 0.96 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{CausalForest\\\\$K{=}4$, $n{=}500$}}\n & Naive & 0.208 & 0.231 & 0.44 \\\\\n & Global & 0.191 & 0.244 & 0.58 \\\\\n & EC-HTE Forest & \\textbf{0.039} & 0.180 & 0.90 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Actionable Diagnostic Without EC-HTE}\n\\label{sec:diagnostic}\n\nProposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts} provides a $\\Delta\\tau$-free diagnostic: global correction increases bias in subgroup $s$ iff $|\\delta_s| / ((1-2\\bar{\\pi}) \\cdot \\pi_s) > 1$, computable from a pilot sample without estimating treatment effects.\nA threshold classifier achieves AUC = 0.86 at the subgroup level (Figure~\\ref{fig:roc}, Appendix).\nOn real LLM confusion matrices (16 combinations; Appendix~\\ref{app:screening}), AUC drops to 0.63; restricting to subgroups with $\\tau_s > 0$ recovers AUC $= 1.0$.\nFor $K{=}2$, $|\\delta_0|{=}|\\delta_1|$ limits discrimination; a meta-criterion ($\\max_s \\mathrm{score}(s) > 1$) recovers reliability (AUC $\\geq 0.75$) for $K{\\geq}4$, while $\\max_s \\mathrm{score}(s) \\leq 1$ signals unreliable regimes (Appendix~\\ref{app:screening}).\n\nThe condition number $\\kappa(\\hat{C}_s)$ governs correction at three levels: $\\kappa > 100$ triggers the singularity guard; $\\kappa \\leq 1.25$ guarantees strongest bias reduction; $\\kappa \\in (1.25, 3]$---covering all 13 tested LLM--dataset combinations---maintains calibrated coverage ($\\geq$93\\% vs.\\ Global 86--91\\%) with RMSE gains at $n_{\\text{expert}} \\geq 500$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_analysis}).\nBudget crossover curves, $K{=}8$ scalability, and multi-LLM validation ($R^2 = 0.975$) appear in Appendices~\\ref{sec:budget}--\\ref{app:multilm}.",
