    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nEC-HTE requires $n_{\\text{expert}} \\times P(Z_{\\text{minority}}) / K \\geq 25$ per subgroup for stable hierarchical Bayesian estimation.\nFor $K{=}2$, EC-HTE remains effective even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:classimbalance}); for $K{=}4$, $P(Z{=}1) \\geq 0.3$ is needed to approach breakeven with global correction at $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}250$.\nEven above this threshold, stratification incurs a variance cost: at $K{=}4$, extreme heterogeneity, and $n_{\\text{expert}} \\leq 100$, CausalForest RMSE reaches 3.5$\\times$ that of the naive estimator.\nThe method also requires moderate baseline classifier accuracy per subgroup; when any subgroup's FNR or FPR approaches 0.5, the confusion matrix row approaches a uniform distribution and inversion amplifies noise (Appendix~\\ref{app:semireal}), as confirmed independently in the hate speech case study (Appendix~\\ref{app:hatespeech}).\nThese failure modes interact: high per-subgroup error-rate variance combined with low minority prevalence $P(Z{=}1)$ jointly reduces effective per-subgroup expert labels for the positive class, compounding both sources of estimation error.\nAdditionally, our main semi-synthetic experiments use constant treatment effects across subgroups; a supplementary heterogeneous-$\\tau$ DGP (Appendix~\\ref{app:multilm_opensource}) shows that EC-HTE achieves targeted bias and RMSE improvement in subgroups with large $\\Delta\\tau$, though aggregate RMSE remains comparable to global correction.\nAt $K{=}8$ with $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}250$ (${\\approx}31$ labels/subgroup), EC-HTE maintains nominal coverage (95.6\\%) but RMSE exceeds global correction, establishing $K{=}8$ as a scalability boundary under fixed budget (Appendix~\\ref{app:k8_subgroups}).\nThe hierarchical prior implicitly performs soft merging---subgroups with insufficient data are shrunk toward the global estimate---but explicit hierarchical subgroup merging or adaptive budget allocation may extend the applicability frontier beyond $K{=}8$; we leave these extensions to future work.\n\nAssumption~(A1) (conditional independence of misclassification and potential outcomes) is not empirically testable.\nWhen LLM accuracy correlates with outcome magnitude---e.g., extreme sentiment is both easier to classify and associated with larger treatment effects---Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity} acquires an $O(\\gamma)$ perturbation (Section~\\ref{sec:theory}).\nSensitivity simulation (Appendix~\\ref{app:a1_sensitivity}) shows that EC-HTE maintains near-nominal coverage ($\\geq$93.5\\%) under outcome-dependent misclassification up to $\\gamma = 0.2$, with bias increasing only 13.7\\%; developing a formal closed-form bound for the $O(\\gamma)$ perturbation remains a natural extension~\\cite{lash2009applying,dorn2022sharp}.\nTreatment-dependent misclassification ($P(\\hat{Z} \\mid Z, T) \\neq P(\\hat{Z} \\mid Z)$), where LLM accuracy varies with treatment status, violates (A1) analogously; the $O(\\gamma)$ perturbation structure applies symmetrically, and sensitivity analysis for this case is left to future work.\n\nOracle experiments confirm CausalForest plug-in SE is well-calibrated (94--96\\% coverage with true $Z$ labels); EC-HTE plug-in SE achieves 93.8\\% aggregate coverage under standard hyperparameters ($n_{\\text{estimators}}{=}200$), with residual under-coverage concentrated in high-error subgroups due to asymmetric $M^{-1}$ SE propagation.\nA full bootstrap procedure ($B{=}100$; re-estimate CM and re-fit per replicate) recovers $\\geq$95\\% coverage across all expert budgets tested ($n_{\\text{expert}} \\in \\{25, 50, 75, 100, 200\\}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_se}); even plug-in SE achieves $\\geq$91\\% at $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}25$.\nFor the DIM estimator, plug-in SE achieves $\\geq$95\\% coverage across all budgets.\n\nThe diagnostic (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}) achieves AUC $= 0.86$ under synthetic symmetric errors but degrades to $0.63$ on real LLM confusion matrices (pooled across 16 model--dataset combinations; Appendix~\\ref{app:screening}).\nFor $K{=}2$, the diagnostic anti-predicts because $|\\delta_0|{=}|\\delta_1|$ forces score differentiation onto $1/\\pi_s$ alone, which inversely correlates with actual harm; for binary $Z$, the asymmetric $B_s$-based formulation (Remark~2) is algebraically equivalent and offers no improvement.\nPerformance is strongest for $K{\\geq}4$ with moderate asymmetry (AUC $= 0.66$; Appendix~\\ref{app:asymmetric}).\nThe diagnostic remains a theoretically grounded criterion---the bias identity's product structure ensures it correctly identifies the \\emph{direction} of bias amplification---but should be paired with $R^2$ validation of the bias identity rather than used as a standalone screening rule.\n\nAll main experiments use symmetric error rates and additive CATE injection ($Y(1) = Y(0) + \\tau(Z)$).\nThe bias identity (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}) generalizes to asymmetric confusion matrices (Appendix~\\ref{app:asymmetric}, $R^2 = 0.991$) and derives from the confusion matrix structure rather than the outcome model.\nWhile our main experiments use additive CATE injection, a supplementary experiment with multiplicative effects ($Y(1) = Y(0) \\times \\exp(\\tau(Z))$) yields comparable bias reduction (52\\%) and nominal coverage (0.955; Appendix~\\ref{app:rlearner}), confirming robustness to the functional form of treatment effects.\nEC-HTE further requires discrete, pre-defined subgroups; subgroup definitions should be pre-specified based on domain knowledge, as data-driven subgroup discovery risks overfitting the confusion matrix structure to sample-specific noise. Continuous effect modifiers or latent subgroup discovery settings require different approaches; discretizing a continuous modifier introduces grouping bias whose magnitude depends on bin width, and intersectional subgroups with many levels may exceed the $n_{\\text{expert}} \\times P(Z_{\\text{minority}}) / K \\geq 25$ budget threshold.\nOur experiments define subgroups by text features (hashtag presence, text length); demographic subgroups---where prior work documents the most concerning disparities \\citep{ashwin2025bias}---require sensitive data access beyond the scope of this study.\nEC-HTE's correction mechanism is agnostic to subgroup definition: it corrects any per-subgroup confusion-matrix heterogeneity, whether defined by text features, demographics, or other covariates.\nGround-truth subgroup CATEs are unavailable in public datasets; classic causal inference benchmarks (e.g., LaLonde, IHDP) lack the LLM annotation step that creates the measurement error our method corrects, precluding direct application. We use synthetic CATE injection on real text features and real LLM annotations.\nWe leave full real-data validation---on a dataset combining RCT design, ground-truth CATEs, and LLM annotations---to future work as suitable public benchmarks become available.\nCode and data generation scripts will be released upon acceptance.",

The appendix probably uses `\appendix` which resets the section counter to use letters (A, B, C, ..., Z). After Z (26 sections), LaTeX runs out of letters and throws "Counter too large". The new section would be the 27th+ appendix section.


Counting through the appendix sections, I'm seeing there are already 16 sections listed, and the problematic "Policy Reversal Zone Analysis" section would be the 17th, which exceeds the 26-letter limit when combined with other appendix sections already in the document.

The solution is to nest it as a subsection under an existing section rather than create a new top-level one. I could place it under "Robustness to Assumption Violations" since that's the final section, or alternatively under "Applicability Screening" depending on which makes the most thematic sense. I'll keep the `\subsection` approach and maintain the `app:policy_reversal` label since it works fine with subsections, so I can place this under the "Robustness to Assumption Violations" section without any cross-referencing issues.

  "content": "[Director 指令] [Reframe] EMNLP 2026 ARR，DDL 5/25。\n\n**Framing 已调整，需要一轮完整写作 pass 确保新 narrative 贯穿全文。**\n\n### 新 Narrative\n\n**核心转型**：从 \"我们提出 EC-HTE 方法\" → \"我们证明标准做法在特定条件下会逆转因果结论，并给出精确判断条件\"\n\n- **Title**: \"When Debiasing Hurts: An Exact Bias Identity for LLM-Annotated Treatment Effects\"\n- **C1（主贡献）**: Exact finite-population bias identity (Theorem 1) — 解释 when and why global correction reverses CATE sign\n- **C2**: Actionable diagnostic (Proposition 2) — 仅从 error rates 判断 correction 是否有害，不需要估 treatment effect\n- **C3（附属）**: Per-subgroup correction prescribed by the identity (EC-HTE) — 不是独立方法，是 identity 的自然推论\n\n**已完成的修改**：\n- abstract.tex: 已 reframe，去掉 \"EC-HTE\" 方法名，改为 \"the per-subgroup correction prescribed by the identity\"，~171 words\n- introduction.tex: contributions list 已 reorder（identity > diagnostic > EC-HTE），EC-HTE 段落已降格\n- conclusion.tex: 已 reframe，从 identity 出发\n- main.tex: 标题已改\n\n**需要你做的**：\n1. 通读全文（abstract/intro/related work/theory/method/experiments/conclusion/limitations），确保新 narrative 在每个章节都一致\n2. 特别关注：\n   - method.tex 的 section title 和开头：不应该暗示 EC-HTE 是主贡献，而是 \"The Correction Prescribed by the Identity\"\n   - experiments.tex：确保实验叙事围绕 \"验证 identity + 验证 diagnostic\" 展开，EC-HTE 评估作为 \"identity prescribes this correction, and we verify it works\"\n   - related work：定位应该是 \"no prior work provides an exact bias identity for heterogeneous misclassification in HTE\"\n3. 写作质量：确保专业、简洁、无 AI 痕迹（no \"notably\", \"crucially\", \"it is worth noting\"）\n4. 新数据整合：intro L17 已改为 \"14 real LLM--dataset combinations\"（TweetEval 11 + CivilComments 3）\n5. 页面限制：主文本必须 ≤ 8 页（当前刚好 8 页，零余量）\n\n**实验完成度**：核心实验全部完成（36 个成功），新增 CivilComments 验证（R²=0.944）。Exp B (stance detection) 仍在跑。\n\n**引用不需要验证**（时间紧）。\n\n编译确认无 error 后向我汇报，我来决定是否提交审稿。"

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Standard confusion-matrix correction for noisy annotations assumes uniform error rates.
3	When this assumption fails---as it routinely does with LLM classifiers, whose error rates vary by up to $5\times$ across demographic subgroups---correction can reverse the sign of estimated causal effects, concluding a beneficial treatment is harmful.
4	We derive an exact bias identity for this setting: global correction introduces subgroup-level bias with a product form $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau / (1 - 2\bar{\pi})$, factoring into each subgroup's deviation from the pooled error rate, the treatment effect heterogeneity, and an amplification factor from matrix inversion.
5	This population-level identity---verified at $R^2 \geq 0.97$ across synthetic grids and 14 real LLM--dataset combinations---predicts that global correction reverses CATE sign in 14.9\% of configurations and amplifies bias in all eight real LLMs tested.
6	The identity yields a diagnostic: a condition on per-subgroup error rates alone predicts when correction increases bias, without estimating any treatment effects (AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity).
7	The per-subgroup correction prescribed by the identity reduces $|\text{bias}|$ by $7.4\times$ via hierarchical Bayesian estimation; the advantage requires sufficient error heterogeneity and expert budget.
8	\end{abstract}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KwShr5jUvRfjX6FhFf6Mk7`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:intro}
3	
4	Consider estimating whether a content moderation policy reduces hate speech differently across demographic groups, using GPT-4 to classify thousands of posts.
5	After standard confusion-matrix correction, the analysis concludes the policy \emph{increases} hate speech among younger users---but the true effect is protective.
6	This sign reversal is not an edge case.
7	
8	Large language models (LLMs) now routinely replace human annotators in computational social science (CSS), producing labels for sentiment, hate speech, stance, and political discourse at costs orders of magnitude below manual coding~\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp}.
9	When these labels serve as effect modifiers in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) analysis, the standard correction protocol\footnote{Throughout, ``debiasing'' refers to correction of annotation measurement-error bias, not algorithmic fairness debiasing.} is straightforward: validate a sample against expert annotations, estimate a confusion matrix, and apply its inverse uniformly to correct conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimates.
10	Yet LLM annotations are not uniformly accurate: \citet{ashwin2025bias} document misclassification rates varying by up to 5$\times$ across demographic subgroups defined by refugee status, gender, and education level; in our pilot study, GPT-4.1 achieves 85.8\% accuracy on TweetEval sentiment, but error rates range from 10.2\% to 20.0\% across text-feature subgroups ($\Delta\pi = 0.098$).
11	
12	We show that this standard correction can make things worse.
13	In 14.9\% of our synthetic configurations (across a grid of $\Delta\pi$ and $\tau(0)$ values; $\tau(0) = 0.03$), global correction reverses the sign of estimated CATE---a protective treatment ($\tau = +0.03$) appears harmful ($\hat{\tau} = -0.059$)---rising to 31\% at $\tau(0) = 0.01$ (Figure~\ref{fig:sign_reversal}); all eight real LLMs tested amplify bias under global correction, while EC-HTE eliminates sign reversal entirely.
14	Global correction amplifies bias by up to 5.9$\times$ ($K{=}4$ with CausalForest); the classical Frazis-Loeb scalar attenuation correction~\cite{frazis2003estimating} \emph{increases} bias by up to 2.9$\times$.
15	The mechanism is over-correction: for subgroups with near-zero misclassification, the naive estimator is nearly unbiased, yet pooled correction calibrated for the average error rate introduces substantial bias precisely where labels were most trustworthy.
16	
17	We derive an exact bias identity explaining when and why this occurs (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}): the correction-induced bias in each subgroup factors into three independently estimable components, verified empirically at $R^2 \geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and 14 real LLM--dataset combinations spanning open-source and proprietary model families.
18	The identity yields a diagnostic (Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}) that predicts when global correction will harm a subgroup using only per-subgroup misclassification rates, without estimating any treatment effects (AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity; Section~\ref{sec:diagnostic}).
19	
20	The per-subgroup correction prescribed by the identity---implemented via hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation (EC-HTE)---reduces $|\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.008 under extreme heterogeneity ($K{=}2$).
21	The advantage is conditional: when $\Delta\pi$ is small, the variance cost of stratification outweighs its bias benefit.
22	
23	Existing corrections---PPI~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi}, DSL~\cite{egami2024dsl}, PPCI~\cite{sui2026ppci}, arm-specific PPI~\cite{mozer2026ppi}, ForestIV~\cite{bu2023forestiv}---assume homogeneous prediction error; none addresses effect modifier misclassification (Section~\ref{sec:related}).
24	
25	\begin{figure*}[t]
26	\centering
27	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig1_sign_reversal.pdf}
28	\caption{CATE sign reversal under global correction ($K{=}2$, $\tau(0) = 0.03$). (A) Naive vs.\ global-corrected CATE; red triangles mark sign reversals (7.5\% of subgroup estimates). (B) Extreme case: true $\tau = +0.03$, estimated $\hat{\tau} = -0.059$. (C) 14.9\% of configurations exhibit sign reversal; all 8 real LLMs amplify bias; EC-HTE: 0\% reversal.}
29	\label{fig:sign_reversal}
30	\end{figure*}
31	
32	Our contributions are:
33	\begin{itemize}
34	    \item An exact finite-population bias identity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}): standard confusion-matrix correction---the default in PPI, DSL, and CSS practice---introduces subgroup-level bias with multiplicative structure $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau / (1-2\bar{\pi})$, explaining when and why correction reverses CATE sign (14.9\% of configurations at $\tau(0) = 0.03$; 31\% at $\tau(0) = 0.01$), verified at $R^2 \geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids, 14 real LLM--dataset combinations, and demographic subgroups (Sections~\ref{sec:theory},~\ref{sec:bias_mechanism}).
35	    \item An actionable diagnostic (Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}): a condition on per-subgroup misclassification rates alone predicts when global correction increases bias, without estimating any treatment effects (AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity)---independently useful without adopting any specific correction method (Section~\ref{sec:diagnostic}).
36	    \item The per-subgroup correction prescribed by the identity, implemented via hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation (EC-HTE)---compatible with difference-in-means, CausalForest, and R-learner, and composable with PPCI for joint covariate/outcome error (Sections~\ref{sec:method}--\ref{sec:diagnostic}).
37	\end{itemize}
38	The bias identity applies beyond LLM annotation to any proxy classifier with subgroup-varying error rates; survey coding, medical diagnosis, and satellite imagery classification share this structure.
39	
```

      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nConsider estimating whether a content moderation policy reduces hate speech differently across demographic groups, using GPT-4 to classify thousands of posts.\nAfter standard confusion-matrix correction, the analysis concludes the policy \\emph{increases} hate speech among younger users---but the true effect is protective.\nThis sign reversal is not an edge case.\n\nLarge language models (LLMs) now routinely replace human annotators in computational social science (CSS), producing labels for sentiment, hate speech, stance, and political discourse at costs orders of magnitude below manual coding~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp}.\nWhen these labels serve as effect modifiers in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) analysis, the standard correction protocol\\footnote{Throughout, ``debiasing'' refers to correction of annotation measurement-error bias, not algorithmic fairness debiasing.} is straightforward: validate a sample against expert annotations, estimate a confusion matrix, and apply its inverse uniformly to correct conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimates.\nYet LLM annotations are not uniformly accurate: \\citet{ashwin2025bias} document misclassification rates varying by up to 5$\\times$ across demographic subgroups defined by refugee status, gender, and education level; in our pilot study, GPT-4.1 achieves 85.8\\% accuracy on TweetEval sentiment, but error rates range from 10.2\\% to 20.0\\% across text-feature subgroups ($\\Delta\\pi = 0.098$).\n\nWe show that this standard correction can make things worse.\nIn 14.9\\% of our synthetic configurations (across a grid of $\\Delta\\pi$ and $\\tau(0)$ values; $\\tau(0) = 0.03$), global correction reverses the sign of estimated CATE---a protective treatment ($\\tau = +0.03$) appears harmful ($\\hat{\\tau} = -0.059$)---rising to 31\\% at $\\tau(0) = 0.01$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:sign_reversal}); all eight real LLMs tested amplify bias under global correction, while EC-HTE eliminates sign reversal entirely.\nGlobal correction amplifies bias by up to 5.9$\\times$ ($K{=}4$ with CausalForest); the classical Frazis-Loeb scalar attenuation correction~\\cite{frazis2003estimating} \\emph{increases} bias by up to 2.9$\\times$.\nThe mechanism is over-correction: for subgroups with near-zero misclassification, the naive estimator is nearly unbiased, yet pooled correction calibrated for the average error rate introduces substantial bias precisely where labels were most trustworthy.\n\nWe derive an exact bias identity explaining when and why this occurs (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}): the correction-induced bias in each subgroup factors into three independently estimable components, verified empirically at $R^2 \\geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and 14 real LLM--dataset combinations spanning open-source and proprietary model families.\nThe identity yields a diagnostic (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}) that predicts when global correction will harm a subgroup using only per-subgroup misclassification rates, without estimating any treatment effects (AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity; Section~\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\n\nThe per-subgroup correction prescribed by the identity---implemented via hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation (EC-HTE)---reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.008 under extreme heterogeneity ($K{=}2$).\nThe advantage is conditional: when $\\Delta\\pi$ is small, the variance cost of stratification outweighs its bias benefit.\n\nExisting corrections---PPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi}, DSL~\\cite{egami2024dsl}, PPCI~\\cite{sui2026ppci}, arm-specific PPI~\\cite{mozer2026ppi}, ForestIV~\\cite{bu2023forestiv}---assume homogeneous prediction error; none addresses effect modifier misclassification (Section~\\ref{sec:related}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_sign_reversal.pdf}\n\\caption{CATE sign reversal under global correction ($K{=}2$, $\\tau(0) = 0.03$). (A) Naive vs.\\ global-corrected CATE; red triangles mark sign reversals (7.5\\% of subgroup estimates). (B) Extreme case: true $\\tau = +0.03$, estimated $\\hat{\\tau} = -0.059$. (C) 14.9\\% of configurations exhibit sign reversal; all 8 real LLMs amplify bias; EC-HTE: 0\\% reversal.}\n\\label{fig:sign_reversal}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item An exact finite-population bias identity (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}): standard confusion-matrix correction---the default in PPI, DSL, and CSS practice---introduces subgroup-level bias with multiplicative structure $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau / (1-2\\bar{\\pi})$, explaining when and why correction reverses CATE sign (14.9\\% of configurations at $\\tau(0) = 0.03$; 31\\% at $\\tau(0) = 0.01$), verified at $R^2 \\geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids, 14 real LLM--dataset combinations, and demographic subgroups (Sections~\\ref{sec:theory},~\\ref{sec:bias_mechanism}).\n    \\item An actionable diagnostic (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}): a condition on per-subgroup misclassification rates alone predicts when global correction increases bias, without estimating any treatment effects (AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity)---independently useful without adopting any specific correction method (Section~\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\n    \\item The per-subgroup correction prescribed by the identity, implemented via hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation (EC-HTE)---compatible with difference-in-means, CausalForest, and R-learner, and composable with PPCI for joint covariate/outcome error (Sections~\\ref{sec:method}--\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\n\\end{itemize}\nThe bias identity applies beyond LLM annotation to any proxy classifier with subgroup-varying error rates; survey coding, medical diagnosis, and satellite imagery classification share this structure.\n",

```text
1	\section{EC-HTE: Error-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} identifies per-subgroup misclassification deviation as the bias source; the natural correction is per-subgroup confusion matrix inversion.
5	EC-HTE corrects subgroup-level misclassification bias in three stages: estimate per-subgroup confusion matrices via hierarchical Bayes, invert them to correct observed outcome moments, and propagate correction uncertainty into downstream causal estimators.
6	The complete procedure is summarized in Algorithm~\ref{alg:echte} (Appendix~\ref{app:algorithm}).
7	
8	\subsection{Hierarchical Bayes Confusion Matrix Estimation}
9	
10	We place a Dirichlet-Multinomial hierarchical model over the confusion matrices $\{C_s\}_{s=1}^K$.
11	For each subgroup $s$, row $j$ of $C_s$ has a Dirichlet prior centered at the global pooled confusion matrix $\hat{C}_{\text{global}}$: $\text{Dir}(\alpha_0 \cdot \hat{C}_{\text{global},j})$, where $\hat{C}_{\text{global},j}$ is the $j$-th row of the pooled CM and $\alpha_0 > 0$ is a shared concentration parameter estimated via empirical Bayes (marginal likelihood maximization).
12	Large $\alpha_0$ pulls subgroup estimates toward $\hat{C}_{\text{global}}$ (global correction), while small $\alpha_0$ allows subgroup-specific rates to dominate (stratified correction).
13	This partial pooling---a form of James-Stein shrinkage well-established in small-area estimation---borrows strength across subgroups when estimating confusion matrices from limited expert labels, reducing variance for small subgroups while preserving heterogeneity where data support it.
14	
15	Posterior inference uses centered empirical Bayes optimization ($<$5ms for $K{=}4$, $N{=}5{,}000$); a full Gibbs MCMC alternative requires $\sim$5s (convergence diagnostics in Appendix~\ref{app:algorithm}).
16	Bootstrap SE ($B{=}100$) adds $\sim$0.4s.
17	HB estimation outperforms stratified MLE in 87.5\% of configurations (21 of 24) across the experimental grid (Section~\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval}), with the largest gains when $n_{\text{expert}} / K < 100$.
18	The advantage requires $n_{\text{expert}} \times P(Z_{\text{minority}}) / K \geq 25$ per subgroup; below this threshold, stratified MLE is preferred because the hierarchical model lacks sufficient observations for stable shrinkage.
19	At $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 1000$, HB and stratified MLE converge to within 0.5\% on all metrics.
20	Sensitivity analysis shows $<$5\% RMSE variation under 2$\times$ hyperprior perturbation, confirming that results are not driven by prior specification.
21	
22	\subsection{Subgroup-Stratified Correction}
23	
24	Given posterior mean confusion matrices $\hat{C}_s$, we correct observed outcome moments per subgroup by matrix inversion:
25	\begin{equation}
26	\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_s = \hat{C}_s^{-1} \cdot \hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_s,
27	\end{equation}
28	where $\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_s = [\hat{\tau}_{\text{obs}}(0, s),\, \hat{\tau}_{\text{obs}}(1, s)]^\top$ collects the observed conditional outcome moments in subgroup $s$.
29	This requires $\det(\hat{C}_s) \neq 0$ for all $s$, which holds under assumption~(A4) ($\pi_s \neq 0.5$) but may fail in finite samples when error rates approach degeneracy.
30	In practice, Laplace smoothing of the confusion matrix counts ensures finite-sample invertibility.
31	We impose a singularity guard: if the condition number $\kappa(\hat{C}_s) > 100$ (for a $2{\times}2$ symmetric confusion matrix, this corresponds to $\pi_s > 0.495$, i.e., near-random classification), subgroup $s$ falls back to global correction $\bar{C}^{-1}$; when the fallback triggers, the standard error computation also uses $\bar{C}^{-1}$ for that subgroup, ensuring consistency between point estimates and confidence intervals.
32	This hard guard prevents numerical catastrophe; $\kappa \leq 1.25$ is a sufficient condition for strongest bias reduction (Appendix~\ref{sec:budget}), though EC-HTE maintains calibrated coverage ($\geq$93\%) across the wider $\kappa \in [1.4, 3.0]$ range observed in practice (Appendix~\ref{app:kappa_analysis}).
33	
34	The contrast with global correction is direct.
35	Global correction applies $\bar{C}^{-1}$ uniformly to all subgroups, introducing the $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau$ bias identified in Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}.
36	Subgroup-stratified correction eliminates this bias by matching each subgroup's correction to its own misclassification structure, at the cost of higher variance from estimating $K$ separate confusion matrices (Appendix~\ref{sec:budget}).
37	
38	\subsection{Variance-Adjusted CausalForest}
39	
40	For heterogeneous treatment effect estimation, we feed corrected covariates into CausalForestDML~\cite{wager2018causal} with honest splitting.
41	The forest produces per-observation standard error estimates $\text{se}_i$.
42	To account for confusion matrix correction, we propagate uncertainty through the inverse mapping:
43	\begin{equation}
44	\text{se}_{\text{corrected},i} = \sqrt{\text{diag}\!\left(\hat{C}_{S_i}^{-1}\, \text{diag}(\text{se}_i^2)\, \hat{C}_{S_i}^{-\top}\right)}.
45	\end{equation}
46	This plug-in SE conditions on $\hat{C}_s$, neglecting CM estimation uncertainty; at $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 100$ this is negligible, but below this threshold a full bootstrap ($B{=}100$; re-estimate CM and re-fit per replicate) maintains $\geq$95\% coverage across all budgets tested ($n_{\text{expert}} \in \{25, 50, 75, 100, 200\}$); even plug-in SE achieves $\geq$91\% at $n_{\text{expert}}{=}25$ (Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_se}).
47	When $n_{\text{expert}}/K < 50$, we recommend bootstrap SE; otherwise plug-in SE suffices.
48	
49	\paragraph{Diagnostic criterion.}
50	Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts} provides a $\Delta\tau$-free diagnostic: global correction harms subgroup $s$ if and only if $|\delta_s| / ((1-2\bar{\pi}) \cdot \pi_s) > 1$, a condition depending only on per-subgroup misclassification rates estimable from a pilot sample.
51	This breaks the circular dependency that would arise if the diagnostic required CATE estimates.
52	A threshold classifier achieves AUC~=~0.86 at the subgroup level, reaching 1.0 when restricted to subgroups with $\tau_s > 0$ (Section~\ref{sec:diagnostic}).
53	
54	\paragraph{Composition with PPCI.}
55	EC-HTE corrects structured covariate misclassification through $\hat{C}_s^{-1}$ inversion but cannot address outcome-level measurement error.
56	PPCI~\cite{sui2026ppci} corrects residual outcome bias using a debiasing estimator but assumes correct covariate labels.
57	The two methods are complementary: apply EC-HTE first to correct covariate labels, then PPCI to correct outcome predictions.
58	Neither is redundant with the other: EC-HTE alone cannot fix outcome error, while PPCI alone incurs higher variance for covariate error.
59	Under both error types simultaneously, the composition reduces $|\text{bias}|$ from 0.179 to 0.007 (Appendix~\ref{sec:ppci}).
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	\subsection{Setup}
4	
5	We test on synthetic ($N{=}5{,}000$, $K \in \{2, 4\}$, controlled $\Delta\tau$ and $\Delta\pi$, 100 MC runs) and semi-synthetic (TweetEval sentiment~\cite{barbieri2020tweeteval}, $K{=}4$ unbalanced subgroups, $\pi \in \{0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.25\}$) DGPs; full specifications in Appendix~\ref{app:dgp}.
6	
7	We compare: (1) \textit{Naive} (no correction), (2) \textit{Global} (pooled confusion matrix, equivalent to PPI/DSL~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,egami2024dsl} with homogeneous error), (3) \textit{Stratified MLE} (per-subgroup DSL without shrinkage), (4) \textit{HB EC-HTE} (our method), (5) \textit{PPCI}~\cite{sui2026ppci}, and (6) \textit{EC-HTE+PPCI}.
8	For CausalForest~\cite{wager2018causal}, we compare naive, global, and EC-HTE variants.
9	Primary metrics are $|\text{bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\% CI coverage, averaged across subgroups and 100 MC runs.
10	Robustness validation covers hate speech, six OpenAI models, subgroup misspecification, asymmetric confusion matrices, confounded treatment, outcome-dependent misclassification (all $\geq$93.5\% coverage), and treatment-dependent misclassification ($\approx$90\% at $\gamma_T \leq 0.05$; Appendices~\ref{app:hatespeech}--\ref{app:treatment_dep}).
11	
12	\subsection{Empirical Validation of the Bias Identity}
13	\label{sec:bias_mechanism}
14	
15	We verify Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} on a $K{=}2$ grid (520 configurations, $\Delta\pi \in [0, 0.25]$, $\Delta\tau \in (0, 0.50]$) and a $K{=}4$ grid (Appendix~\ref{app:dgp}).
16	Regressing observed subgroup bias on $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau / (1{-}2\bar{\pi})$ yields $R^2 = 0.994$ ($K{=}2$) and $0.974$ ($K{=}4$); residual variance reflects MC noise ($R^2 \geq 0.976$ even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$; Appendix~\ref{app:classimbalance}).
17	Global correction worsens bias in 50\% of $K{=}2$ configurations (260/520, excluding $\Delta\pi{=}0$), with amplification reaching 2.86$\times$ ($K{=}2$) and 2.41$\times$ ($K{=}4$); Figure~\ref{fig:heatmap} visualizes the amplification surface.
18	Beyond amplification, global correction reverses the sign of estimated CATE---concluding a protective treatment is harmful---in 14.9\% of configurations at $\tau(0) = 0.03$ (31\% at $\tau(0) = 0.01$; critical threshold $\tau(0) < 0.089$, matching Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}), with all eight real LLMs amplifying bias under global correction; EC-HTE eliminates sign reversal entirely (Figure~\ref{fig:sign_reversal}; Appendix~\ref{app:policy_reversal}).
19	
20	\subsection{EC-HTE Evaluation}
21	\label{sec:ec_hte_eval}
22	
23	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} presents the main results across representative configurations.
24	In the $K{=}2$ extreme row, global correction \emph{increases} $|\text{bias}|$ relative to naive (0.059 vs.\ 0.058), confirming the over-correction mechanism of Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}.
25	F-L scalar correction increases $|\text{bias}|$ to 0.122 (coverage 0.68); F-L per-subgroup to 0.168 as $(1{-}2\pi_s) \to 0$---both worse than naive (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}).
26	HB EC-HTE reduces $|\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.008 (7.4$\times$) at $K{=}2$ and from 0.056 to 0.012 (4.7$\times$) at $K{=}4$, maintaining near-nominal coverage (0.94--0.96) with lower RMSE than stratified MLE.
27	Under moderate heterogeneity and in TweetEval ($K{=}4$), methods converge ($p > 0.05$); EC-HTE incurs higher RMSE when $\Delta\pi$ is small (Table~\ref{tab:main_supp}).
28	At $K{=}8$ ($n_{\text{expert}}/K{=}31$), coverage remains $\geq$95.6\% but RMSE increases; we recommend $n_{\text{expert}}/K \geq 50$ (Appendix~\ref{app:k8_subgroups}).
29	
30	\paragraph{CausalForest.}
31	Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} applies to any estimator whose inputs pass through a confusion matrix; CausalForest~\cite{wager2018causal} with a noisy effect modifier $\hat{Z}$ inherits the same $M_{\text{global}}^{-1} M_s$ structure.
32	With CausalForest ($K{=}4$, extreme, $n{=}500$), EC-HTE reduces $|\text{bias}|$ from 0.191 to 0.039 (4.9$\times$) with coverage 0.90; global amplifies bias up to 5.9$\times$ (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}; bootstrap SE in Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_se}).
33	
34	\begin{table}[t]
35	\centering
36	\caption{Main results (extreme heterogeneity). Metrics averaged over subgroups, $z$-levels, 100 MC runs. Bold: lowest $|\text{bias}|$. $^\dagger$RMSE exceeds Global. F-L Scalar: $\hat{\tau}/(1{-}2\bar{\pi})$~\cite{frazis2003estimating}; F-L Per-sub.: per-subgroup variant (binary $Z$ only). Moderate/TweetEval in Table~\ref{tab:main_supp}; MC SEs in Appendix~\ref{app:mcse}.}
37	\label{tab:main_results}
38	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
39	\begin{tabular}{ll ccc}
40	\toprule
41	Configuration & Method & $|\text{Bias}|$ & RMSE & 95\% CI Cov. \\
42	\midrule
43	\multirow{6}{*}{\shortstack[l]{DIM\\$K{=}2$, $n{=}250$}}
44	 & Naive & 0.058 & 0.090 & 0.82 \\
45	 & Global & 0.059 & 0.099 & 0.87 \\
46	 & F-L Scalar & 0.122 & 0.159 & 0.68 \\
47	 & F-L Per-sub. & 0.168 & 0.220 & 0.65 \\
48	 & Strat.\ MLE & 0.012 & 0.094 & 0.95 \\
49	 & HB EC-HTE & \textbf{0.008} & 0.089 & 0.94 \\
50	\midrule
51	\multirow{4}{*}{\shortstack[l]{DIM\\$K{=}4$, $n{=}500$}}
52	 & Naive & 0.062 & 0.113 & 0.88 \\
53	 & Global & 0.056 & 0.125 & 0.91 \\
54	 & Strat.\ MLE & \textbf{0.010} & 0.127$^\dagger$ & 0.95 \\
55	 & HB EC-HTE & 0.012 & 0.120 & 0.96 \\
56	\midrule
57	\multirow{3}{*}{\shortstack[l]{CausalForest\\$K{=}4$, $n{=}500$}}
58	 & Naive & 0.208 & 0.231 & 0.44 \\
59	 & Global & 0.191 & 0.244 & 0.58 \\
60	 & EC-HTE Forest & \textbf{0.039} & 0.180 & 0.90 \\
61	\bottomrule
62	\end{tabular}}
63	\end{table}
64	
65	\subsection{Actionable Diagnostic Without EC-HTE}
66	\label{sec:diagnostic}
67	
68	Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts} provides a $\Delta\tau$-free diagnostic: global correction increases bias in subgroup $s$ iff $|\delta_s| / ((1-2\bar{\pi}) \cdot \pi_s) > 1$, computable from a pilot sample without estimating treatment effects.
69	The diagnostic achieves AUC $= 0.63$ across 16 real LLM--dataset combinations; restricting to subgroups with $\Delta\tau > 0$ recovers AUC $= 1.0$ ($0.86$ on synthetic grids; Figure~\ref{fig:roc}, Appendix~\ref{app:screening}).
70	For $K{=}2$, $|\delta_0|{=}|\delta_1|$ limits discrimination; a meta-criterion ($\max_s \mathrm{score}(s) > 1$) recovers reliability for $K{\geq}4$ (Appendix~\ref{app:screening}).
71	
72	The condition number $\kappa(\hat{C}_s)$ provides a practical guard: $\kappa > 100$ triggers fallback; all 13 tested LLM--dataset combinations fall in $\kappa \in (1.25, 3]$ with calibrated coverage $\geq$93\% (Appendix~\ref{app:kappa_analysis}).
73	Budget crossover, $K{=}8$ scalability, and multi-LLM validation ($R^2 = 0.975$) appear in Appendices~\ref{sec:budget}--\ref{app:multilm}.
```

      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe test on synthetic ($N{=}5{,}000$, $K \\in \\{2, 4\\}$, controlled $\\Delta\\tau$ and $\\Delta\\pi$, 100 MC runs) and semi-synthetic (TweetEval sentiment~\\cite{barbieri2020tweeteval}, $K{=}4$ unbalanced subgroups, $\\pi \\in \\{0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.25\\}$) DGPs; full specifications in Appendix~\\ref{app:dgp}.\n\nWe compare: (1) \\textit{Naive} (no correction), (2) \\textit{Global} (pooled confusion matrix, equivalent to PPI/DSL~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,egami2024dsl} with homogeneous error), (3) \\textit{Stratified MLE} (per-subgroup DSL without shrinkage), (4) \\textit{HB EC-HTE} (our method), (5) \\textit{PPCI}~\\cite{sui2026ppci}, and (6) \\textit{EC-HTE+PPCI}.\nFor CausalForest~\\cite{wager2018causal}, we compare naive, global, and EC-HTE variants.\nPrimary metrics are $|\\text{bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\\% CI coverage, averaged across subgroups and 100 MC runs.\nRobustness validation covers hate speech, six OpenAI models, subgroup misspecification, asymmetric confusion matrices, confounded treatment, outcome-dependent misclassification (all $\\geq$93.5\\% coverage), and treatment-dependent misclassification ($\\approx$90\\% at $\\gamma_T \\leq 0.05$; Appendices~\\ref{app:hatespeech}--\\ref{app:treatment_dep}).\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation of the Bias Identity}\n\\label{sec:bias_mechanism}\n\nWe verify Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity} on a $K{=}2$ grid (520 configurations, $\\Delta\\pi \\in [0, 0.25]$, $\\Delta\\tau \\in (0, 0.50]$) and a $K{=}4$ grid (Appendix~\\ref{app:dgp}).\nRegressing observed subgroup bias on $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau / (1{-}2\\bar{\\pi})$ yields $R^2 = 0.994$ ($K{=}2$) and $0.974$ ($K{=}4$); residual variance reflects MC noise ($R^2 \\geq 0.976$ even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$; Appendix~\\ref{app:classimbalance}).\nGlobal correction worsens bias in 50\\% of $K{=}2$ configurations (260/520, excluding $\\Delta\\pi{=}0$), with amplification reaching 2.86$\\times$ ($K{=}2$) and 2.41$\\times$ ($K{=}4$); Figure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} visualizes the amplification surface.\nBeyond amplification, global correction reverses the sign of estimated CATE---concluding a protective treatment is harmful---in 14.9\\% of configurations at $\\tau(0) = 0.03$ (31\\% at $\\tau(0) = 0.01$; critical threshold $\\tau(0) < 0.089$, matching Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}), with all eight real LLMs amplifying bias under global correction; EC-HTE eliminates sign reversal entirely (Figure~\\ref{fig:sign_reversal}; Appendix~\\ref{app:policy_reversal}).\n\n\\subsection{EC-HTE Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:ec_hte_eval}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} presents the main results across representative configurations.\nIn the $K{=}2$ extreme row, global correction \\emph{increases} $|\\text{bias}|$ relative to naive (0.059 vs.\\ 0.058), confirming the over-correction mechanism of Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}.\nF-L scalar correction increases $|\\text{bias}|$ to 0.122 (coverage 0.68); F-L per-subgroup to 0.168 as $(1{-}2\\pi_s) \\to 0$---both worse than naive (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).\nHB EC-HTE reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.008 (7.4$\\times$) at $K{=}2$ and from 0.056 to 0.012 (4.7$\\times$) at $K{=}4$, maintaining near-nominal coverage (0.94--0.96) with lower RMSE than stratified MLE.\nUnder moderate heterogeneity and in TweetEval ($K{=}4$), methods converge ($p > 0.05$); EC-HTE incurs higher RMSE when $\\Delta\\pi$ is small (Table~\\ref{tab:main_supp}).\nAt $K{=}8$ ($n_{\\text{expert}}/K{=}31$), coverage remains $\\geq$95.6\\% but RMSE increases; we recommend $n_{\\text{expert}}/K \\geq 50$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:k8_subgroups}).\n\n\\paragraph{CausalForest.}\nTheorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity} applies to any estimator whose inputs pass through a confusion matrix;
